D'Ieteren · DIE-BE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 28 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: D'Ieteren

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 50,213 words, set out under Richard's eleven ratings in the order of the eleven ratings page: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the eleven ratings page uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

External headwinds/tailwinds

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0245C-0552C-0738C-0955C-3558C-3952
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-4411th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Intensity is tightening sharply where D'Ieteren is weakest (Belgian new cars), tightening mildly in European glass, and stable or easing in US insured glass, where most of Belron's profit sits. The direction is negative overall, but the tightening is concentrated in the segment with the smallest and shrinking profit weight.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • US insured glass: easing for Belron. Safelite became the third-party claims administrator for State Farm's National Glass Program in July 2025 (State Farm about 17 to 19 percent of US auto insurance); Safelite Solutions handles 65 to 75 percent of US non-collision glass claims; Travelers and Liberty Mutual call it their preferred vendor; experts say regional players lack the coverage national insurers need and no large carrier has diverted volume since January 2026. Boyd reports volume of minus 0 to 2 percent and same-store sales of minus 1 percent; Driven Brands' Auto Glass Now grows 7.2 percent but at a 4.3 percent margin.
  • US cash-pay and adjacent players: mild tightening. Regionals price-match in cash-pay where Safelite holds a 25 percent premium; Boyd bundles collision, glass and calibration into single insurer contracts (Joe Hudson's acquisition, 258 centres); collision networks target commercial segments where Safelite under-indexes.
  • European glass: tightening. Cary Group's Autoglass Clinic doubled its Belgian footprint and holds over 50 percent of the Belgian leasing channel; European calibration prices sit around EUR 150 a job against USD 250 to 700 in the US, because smaller operators win large insurance contracts.
  • Belgian new cars: sharp tightening. D'Ieteren's share fell to about 21.5 percent from about 23.0 percent a year earlier (153 basis points); competitors discount to clear stock; Chinese manufacturers reached 13 percent of European sales in August 2026 (under 8 percent a year earlier) and over 30 percent of plug-in hybrids, untariffed; Volkswagen offers up to EUR 6,000 off its ID range; Inchcape cut its FY2026 margin guidance on 22 October 2026.
  • Parts distribution: share shifting to low-cost players. Inter Cars grows 12 percent on a selling, general and administrative cost ratio of 14.4 percent against LKQ's 28.7 percent. PHE is outgrowing the incumbents, but the low-cost Central European model is the direction of travel.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

B476th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Roughly neutral for the group, with the effect split by bargaining position: Belron converts input inflation into price with a lag and gains relatively from tariffs; Automotive and PHE absorb Belgian and French wage indexation and freight; Automotive's single-supplier exposure to a Volkswagen in restructuring is the one concentration that is getting worse.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Glass procurement costs rose 10 to 20 percent recently (supply disruption and tariffs); Belron passes these through to insurers by semi-annual contract indexation pegged to the NAGS price index; Belron is the largest or second-largest glass buyer (about 10 million units in 2024) and says its supply agreements are insulated from the 85 percent rise in European gas prices in early 2026.
  • Brent at USD 111 (October 2026) raises freight and transport: PHE flagged transport and personnel inflation but held its margin (9.6 percent in H1 2026); TVH had freight inflation and about 20 basis points of tariff cost in H1 2025.
  • Volkswagen's Future Plan 2030 (approved 3 September 2026): 50,000 job cuts, model portfolio halved by 2035, nameplates from 150 to about 70 to 75, European overcapacity of 500,000 vehicles acknowledged, China deliveries minus 37 percent in Q2 2026. A manufacturer under this pressure tends to squeeze distributor terms and reduce the range the distributor can sell.
  • PHE (275 suppliers, top 10 at 40 percent, two to three per product family) and TVH (7,700 suppliers) carry little concentration risk.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

D3814th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Macro bites hardest at the level of the equity, then at the company's reported earnings, and least at Belron's operations. At the equity level the regime (risk-off, rising discount rates, consumer-discretionary and car-retail de-rating) compresses the multiple of a levered, complex holding; at the earnings level currency translation and Belron's floating-rate junk debt act on D'Ieteren more than on the typical European stock; operationally, glass repair is close to GDP-agnostic.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Equity level. The Fitness for Fighting regime (23 October 2026): risk-off broadening, US 10-year near 5.10 percent with a December Fed hike above 90 percent priced, Brent USD 111, French sovereign stress spilling into Italy, defensives leading cyclicals on every window. The tilts: Industry Consumer discretionary Double Underweight, Sector H.14 Underweight (from Neutral), Fragility Double Underweight, Momentum Underweight. D'Ieteren is a multi-part, levered, family-controlled holding: the opposite of the "clean, unfragile" case the regime rewards.
  • Earnings level: currency. Belron's revenue is 55 percent North American, translation largely unhedged. FY2025 currency cost EUR 30.6m of adjusted PBT; H1 2026 carried a 2.2 percent negative translation effect on operating results. Sensitivity: plus or minus 5 percent in EUR/USD is plus or minus EUR 0.27 of EPS (about 2 percent); a 10 percent fall in the dollar cuts group-share EBITDA by about EUR 75m (3.9 percent) but pre-tax profit by only about 3.2 percent because USD debt translation offsets. Guidance assumes 1.17; consensus expects 1.18 by late 2027.
  • Earnings level: rates. Belron's EUR 8.3bn of junk-rated debt; a 100 basis-point rise is about EUR 42m at group share, about EUR 0.6 of EPS (3.4 percent of FY2028 earnings on the prior memo's arithmetic).
  • Operations. Weak US consumer (University of Michigan 46.3) bears on the cash-pay and claims-avoidance leg; high fuel prices could reduce miles driven (Jefferies flags this as a risk to Belron volume); Belgian private demand is depressed by rates and cost of living.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–53

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The single most important other dynamic is positive and new: Belron's private-equity co-owners (CD&R, Hellman & Friedman, GIC, BlackRock, about 40 percent together) need liquidity, and that need, not D'Ieteren's own choice, opened the formal review of a Belron listing on 9 September 2026. Against it sit three negatives: the market is splitting the H.14 sector into parts and services (rewarded) and car retail and leasing (punished), and a mixed group trades with the weaker half in risk-off; the SPDG family branch is a standing seller of about 10 percent; and insurers are consolidating claims administration in a way that helps Belron today but concentrates buyer power.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The review: an Amsterdam listing mooted at EUR 30 to 40bn of value; Rothschild appointed; D'Ieteren intends to keep 50.3 percent; Belron leverage of 4.3x is read by the sell-side as the green light for a 2027 listing.
  • Sector split: the Assistant Portfolio Manager's note of 28 October 2026 says parts distribution, rental and services held up while car retail and leasing broke (Inchcape minus 9.3 percent on 22 October); "for mixed groups such as D'Ieteren, watch which half the shares trade with". D'Ieteren's relative strength is at the 31st percentile and its technical momentum D: the shares are trading with the car-retail half despite most profit sitting in the other.
  • SPDG (Perier branch) is selling its remaining 10.6 percent (or 9.2 percent) over five years from late 2024.
  • Claims administration: Safelite's State Farm mandate routes unaligned claims to its network; the same insurers are the ones capping calibration reimbursement.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C+5610th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Standard entrants are no threat to Belron (the calibration bar rises each year); adjacent entrants (collision networks bundling glass and calibration for insurers) are a medium threat in the US; the orthogonal entrant that matters is the vehicle manufacturer, either by self-calibrating cameras or by keeping ADAS repairs in its dealer network. For Automotive, the orthogonal entrant (Chinese manufacturers, direct and agency distribution) is already the main threat.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Adjacent: Boyd/Glass America, Crash Champions, Caliber package collision with glass and ADAS calibration; Boyd bought Joe Hudson's (258 centres, about USD 750m of sales).
  • Standard: one-person shops cannot fund equipment; mobile static calibration raises the bar further; a former Driven Brands president says the high cost of calibration is driving consolidation.
  • Orthogonal: a JLR calibration head describes "OEMs dominating ADAS repairs amid rising complexity"; insurers (AXA) exploring direct OEM parts sourcing, held back because manufacturers protect dealer loyalty; self-calibration (C-03).
  • Parts: low-cost Central European distributors expanding west; online (PHE owns Oscaro, the leading French online DIY platform).
  • Cars: BYD-style insourcing; Leapmotor and other Chinese brands entering Belgian fleets.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C+5610th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Suppliers to Belron, PHE and TVH are a support (diversified, competing for the largest buyer); the one supplier that is a material source of risk is Volkswagen Group to D'Ieteren Automotive, a sole supplier under a 78-year exclusive contract that is cutting costs, halving its model range and losing share to Chinese rivals.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Glass: Fuyao (domestic US supply from Ohio), AGC (recycling partnership), Xinyi, Saint-Gobain (auto-glass division mooted for sale at 8x EBITDA), Vitro; DuPont adhesives; Bosch calibration equipment. Belron audits suppliers (39 sites, 100 percent of critical suppliers) and holds purchasing power as the largest buyer.
  • Volkswagen: Future Plan 2030 (50,000 jobs, nameplates from 150 to about 70 to 75, 500,000 units of European overcapacity); Q2 2026 group EBIT 10 percent below consensus; Audi deliveries minus 15 percent; Porsche registrations in Belgium minus 38 percent year to date in mid-2026; VW Group EU5 sales minus 5.5 percent in August 2026. A manufacturer in this position seeks distributor-margin savings and may consider agency models.
  • PHE: 275 suppliers, two to three per product family, top ten 40 percent; TVH: 7,700 suppliers.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0160C-0355C-0447C-0872C-3263C-3752C-4362
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

C-5814th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Demand is moving in opposite directions in the two businesses that matter, and the profit-weighted net is a modest improvement over the last six months against the prior twelve to eighteen: a structural, highly visible content tailwind plus a cyclical volume recovery at Belron (57 percent of operating profit), against a structural, company-confirmed decline at D'Ieteren Automotive. The content leg at Belron has two-plus years of visibility; the volume leg does not, and it is the part most exposed to the weakening US consumer over the next six months.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron, structural leg (high visibility). Recalibration penetration rose from 2 percent of jobs in 2017 to 47.2 percent in FY2025 and 52.1 percent in H1 2026 (45.9 percent a year earlier); camera-bracket fitment on windscreens is projected from 49 percent (2025) to 60 percent (2028); EU General Safety Regulation 2 mandates windscreen cameras; recalibration adds about EUR 150 to 300 per job. Bernstein models Belron revenue growth of 5 to 6 percent a year for 2026 to 2029: about 2 percent volume and 3 to 4 percent complexity mix and price. This is the most predictable demand force in the group.
  • Belron, cyclical leg (low visibility). FY2025 organic growth of 5.9 percent had flat underlying volume (5.1 percent price and mix) because US premium inflation of about 40 percent (2022 to 2024) drove claims avoidance; H1 2026 organic growth rose to 7.3 percent with volume +3.9 percent and North America +9.4 percent "on improved claims activity". The recovery is real, but it is the reversal of a consumer-behaviour trough, and the US consumer is now weakening (University of Michigan current conditions at the lowest in the survey's history, October 2026). Glass damage is non-discretionary (UK windscreen claims fell only 11 percent when traffic fell 21 percent), so the downside is a slower volume leg rather than a collapse.
  • D'Ieteren Automotive (structural decline, visible but not cyclical). Belgian registrations fell 7.5 percent in 2025 and 2.4 percent in H1 2026 (225,107 units); the company now expects a plateau at 400,000 units a year, 20 percent below pre-pandemic. Company-car sales fell 10 percent in H1 2026; electric-vehicle purchase subsidies were removed in 2026; mobility budgets from 2027 let employees opt out of company cars; corporate buyers are 70 to 75 percent of the market. The post-pandemic order book went from 58,000 (end-2023) to about 23,500. This is not a cyclical swing with two-year visibility of recovery; it is a lower base.
  • PHE (structural support). European parc age 12.9 years, out-of-warranty vehicles about 84 percent of the parc, the prime six-to-fourteen-year cohort growing 12 percent by 2028; independent aftermarket share of repair 68 percent. PHE organic growth 3.9 percent (FY2025) and 6.0 percent (H1 2026) against a market growing about 2 percent ex inflation and LKQ Europe at minus 5.1 percent in Q2 2026: PHE is gaining share. Parts prices turned from a 3.7 percent decline in 2025 to +1.5 percent quarter on quarter in Q2 2026.
  • TVH (cyclical, recovering). Flat organic growth in North America in FY2025 (adjusted PBT GS minus 26.3 percent), then +7.7 percent at constant currency in H1 2026 on construction; Middle East soft.

Modelling implication. Belron organic growth of mid-to-high single digits, with the volume leg (not the mix leg) as the variable to flex; Automotive revenue down high single digits in FY2026 and roughly flat thereafter on a 400,000-unit market; PHE mid-single-digit organic plus acquisitions; TVH low-to-mid single digits.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

B+624th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The disruption that matters most, vehicle technology, currently acts for the company: rising windscreen complexity is Belron's margin engine. Its mirror image, self-calibrating cameras that would remove the recalibration step, is neither strengthening fast nor disproved: research and development activity rose visibly in 2026, but the engineering evidence still says a replaced windscreen needs a manual baseline recalibration, so the threat is to terminal value after about 2030 rather than to the 18-month forecast. Separately, the disruption of single-manufacturer car distribution (Chinese entrants, agency models, manufacturer insourcing) is strengthening and acts against D'Ieteren Automotive.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • For Belron (acting now). Fuyao launched a fused lidar-and-camera windscreen in 2025; head-up display penetration about 54 percent; forward-collision warning and emergency braking in 94 percent of US new vehicles by 2023, about 100 percent by 2030; Bernstein says the industry is less than halfway through complexity upgrades. Mobile calibration is now 40 to 50 percent of calibrations (Travelers, September 2026) and Autoglass rolled out Mobile Recalibration Pro in the UK in October 2026: the technology shift is raising the capital and skill bar that one-person shops cannot clear.
  • Against Belron (strengthening in R&D, not in deployment). Aptiv's single-chip smart camera entered mass production in September 2026; patent filings by Hyundai, Honda and Stoneridge (October 2026) for automatic pose correction; Tesla vehicles self-calibrate on the road. Against: a JLR calibration head says in-use self-tuning corrects drift but "cannot recover from a full component replacement"; dynamic calibration fails in bad weather and dense traffic; manufacturers avoid full self-calibration for liability reasons; Aptiv and Mobileye filings give no launch dates. The Key Question report rates confidence on this timeline as low.
  • The asymmetry that matters for valuation. The bull case on recalibration is quantified by the sell-side (penetration to 60 percent-plus, margin above 25 percent); the self-calibration bear case exists only as qualitative framing. The Wisdom Library entry "Bear Case Lives in Qualitative Framing" applies: if the market starts to price it, the multiple moves before the earnings do, and the most likely moment is the Belron listing, when new investors price Belron's terminal value.
  • Against Automotive (strengthening). BYD declined to renew Inchcape's Benelux distribution (expiring Q3 2027) and bought its German network from Hedin in 2024: the precedent that manufacturers insource distribution in scaled markets. D'Ieteren's 78-year exclusive Volkswagen contract shields it from this but also bars it from Chinese brands. Electric vehicles need about 40 percent fewer after-sales interventions (a Norwegian study finds after-sales turnover only 7 percent lower).
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F4423rd of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Regulatory change is active on four fronts and nets to a mild negative over the next six to twelve months, because the visible near-term effects fall on Automotive's volumes and on the price Belron can charge for calibration in the US, while the large positive (mandated cameras) is slow-acting and already in the numbers.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Positive, structural: vehicle safety mandates. GSR2 cameras in Europe; Belron lobbies for aftermarket repair-information rights.
  • Negative, forming: US insurer regulation of calibration billing. Progressive is implementing calibration pricing policies; State Farm and GEICO cap reimbursement for subsequent calibrations on one claim; USAA requires pre- and post-scan reports. Not yet visible in Belron's price and mix (still +3.4 percent in H1 2026).
  • Negative, now: Belgian fiscal policy. Company-car deductibility for combustion and hybrid vehicles falls to 0 percent by 2028 (100 percent for battery electric); purchase subsidies removed in 2026; mobility budgets from 2027. These hit Automotive volumes and the higher-margin fleet mix.
  • Mixed: trade. US tariffs of 25 percent on imported glass hurt import-dependent regionals more than Safelite, which sources most US glass from Fuyao's Ohio plant: a relative advantage. EU tariffs on Chinese battery-electric vehicles (up to 35.3 to 38.1 percent) do not cover plug-in hybrids, which leaves Volkswagen's Belgian price position exposed. EU CO2 targets moved to 2025 to 2027 averaging.
  • Sovereign and tax. Goldman Sachs names Belgium's debt dynamics as worrying (16 October 2026), so far one clause; the 30 percent Belgian withholding on the 2024 special dividend triggered retail selling.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

B747th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Strongly, at Belron: the industry-level rise in windscreen technology content is accelerating Belron's revenue per job and its share, and the evidence shows no deceleration yet. The same industry fact (only a minority of eligible vehicles are calibrated across the wider repair market) means the runway is longer for the operator with the capability, not shorter.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Penetration gains: 42.2 percent (FY2024) to 47.2 percent (FY2025), +5.0 points; 45.9 to 52.1 percent year on year in H1 2026, +6.2 points. The company expects more than 60 percent by FY2028.
  • Recalibration drops through at close to 100 percent incremental margin (EBITA margin over 50 percent on Bernstein's figures) and accounted for about 6 of Belron's 17 percentage points of margin expansion since 2018.
  • A former Crash Champions executive (April 2026) says only 38 percent of eligible vehicles are actually calibrated across the industry because independent shops lack equipment: this both caps the industry's terminal rate and shows the share-shift mechanism toward Belron.
  • Parc lag: the average vehicle is about 13 years old, so the camera-equipped share of the parc rises steadily for years; this is the "mechanical content growth" pattern (the same shape the files describe for ASM International's deposition steps per wafer), which sustains above-market growth until saturation.
  • PHE has a slower sector driver (ageing parc, independent share gain); TVH's (material-handling parts about 3 percent a year, automated guided vehicles about 10 percent) is modest.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C+6510th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Customer demand supports the thesis at Belron (non-discretionary, insurer-funded, recovering in volume and rising in value per job) and undermines it only at Automotive. The fragility is specific: the share of glass work paid by insurers falls when deductibles and premiums rise, and the US consumer is now weakening, so the volume recovery of H1 2026 is the least secure part of the demand picture.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron's revenue is 70 to 77 percent from insurer contracts; North American revenue 40 percent insurance, 30 percent cash, 30 percent commercial. Windscreen damage is a safety defect that cannot be deferred indefinitely; demand follows miles driven, weather and road quality.
  • The claims-avoidance episode: US premiums up more than 25 percent (up to 40 percent at some carriers) in 2024 to 2025; US claims fell about 15 percent; drivers chose USD 1,000 deductibles and cash repair at independents where Safelite under-indexes; avoidance eased from H2 2025.
  • PHE: 85 percent B2B to garages (H+2 delivery on 90 percent of SKUs); TVH: customers prioritise machine uptime over part price; 82,000 to 90,000 customers, the largest 1 percent of sales.
  • Automotive: private buyers retreating on rates and cost of living; corporate buyers redirected by tax; a 400,000-unit plateau.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4721st of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Regulators help Belron's volume (camera mandates), threaten Belron's price (insurer-led billing rules in the US, its largest market), and hurt Automotive's volume and mix (Belgian company-car taxation and subsidy withdrawal). The helpful regulation is durable; the harmful ones are forming and could tighten. Net slightly negative over three years, but small at the profit level.

Reasoning and evidence. As set out in C-04: GSR2 cameras; Progressive, State Farm, GEICO and USAA calibration billing rules; Belgian deductibility for combustion and hybrid company cars to 0 percent by 2028, subsidies removed in 2026, mobility budgets in 2027; Euro 7 and CO2 targets forcing Volkswagen's electrification; EU tariffs on Chinese battery-electric vehicles but not plug-in hybrids (safeguard quotas under consideration); US glass tariffs that hurt Belron's import-dependent competitors more than Belron; ETS2 carbon pricing on road fuel from 2027. Belron's guidance excludes trade-policy effects.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F5823rd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Belron's market (about EUR 40bn, of which Belron has about EUR 6.7bn) and PHE's (about EUR 100bn at the European distributor tier) are large relative to the companies, but both grow slowly in volume; mid-term growth comes from content (Belron) and consolidation (PHE), not from the market. Realistic mid-term growth: Belron 5 to 7 percent a year organic to about 2030, slowing as calibration penetration plateaus; PHE 3 to 5 percent organic plus 2 to 5 percent from acquisitions; TVH 3 to 5 percent; Automotive flat to slightly down; group-share revenue about 3 to 5 percent a year, below the 12 to 20 percent band the framework calls ideal.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Glass: parc growth about 3 percent a year globally (1.27 billion vehicles in 2025; a figure the business-foundations digest flags as unverified); Bernstein's Belron algorithm 2 percent volume plus 3 to 4 percent complexity mix and price; Belron's own target above 5 percent organic; share gains of about 0.4 points a year since 2015 add roughly 2 percent of growth.
  • Calibration headroom: penetration 52.1 percent today; target above 60 percent by FY2028; the Key Question report cites forecasts approaching 100 percent of eligible jobs by 2030 for the underlying safety systems, against 38 percent of eligible vehicles actually calibrated industry-wide.
  • Parts: European distributor tier EUR 100bn growing about 2 percent; top seven at 25 percent leaves a long roll-up runway.
  • TVH: material-handling parts growing about 3 percent a year; construction parts 3 to 6 percent.
  • Automotive: a 400,000-unit plateau.
1c

Net external change forces negative/positive

C-5614th of 303 answers
Judgement55C-0662C-1055
Group judgement

External change forces

C-5614th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The external forces are favourable where the profit is and unfavourable where the profit is not, which makes the operating picture better than the share-price picture. Belron enjoys a structural, mandated and still-accelerating content driver (calibration penetration up 6.2 points in a year), a cyclical volume recovery in the US and a competitive structure in its main market that is consolidating toward it; PHE enjoys an ageing parc and share gains against shrinking incumbents. Automotive faces a structural decline that management itself calls permanent, intensified by Chinese entrants and Belgian fiscal change, and its sole supplier is restructuring. The forces that act on the company more than on the universe are currency translation (55 percent of Belron's revenue in North America, unhedged in translation) and the cost of Belron's junk-rated debt in a rising-rate regime; the force that acts on the equity is a risk-off market that is de-rating car retail and levered, complex consumer-discretionary names. The two external forces to monitor because they could change the picture are US insurer pricing behaviour on calibration (the price leg) and the US consumer (the volume leg); the one with the most valuation consequence and the least evidence is self-calibration.

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D6015th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The group's earnings cycle bottomed in FY2025 to H1 2026 and is turning up at segment level, but the reported revenue line will barely show it. FY2026 is the flat year and FY2027 the first year of recovery on consensus. Belron's cycle has turned decisively; TVH has turned; Automotive is at or near its margin trough but not yet at a confirmed floor.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Group-share revenue: FY2023 EUR 11,662m, FY2024 EUR 12,073.9m, FY2025 EUR 12,082.1m, H1 2026 minus 0.4 percent reported (+0.7 percent at constant currency). Adjusted PBT GS: FY2023 EUR 970.8m, FY2024 EUR 1,065.0m, FY2025 EUR 955.6m (minus 10.3 percent, but +3.8 percent at constant currency excluding the extra interest from the 2024 recapitalisation), H1 2026 +6.6 percent.
  • Belron: organic growth 5.9 percent (FY2025), 6.9 percent (Q1 2026), 7.3 percent (H1 2026); operating-profit growth stepped from 5 percent in H1 2025 to 20 percent in H2 2025 and 12.3 percent reported (16.5 percent at constant currency) in H1 2026.
  • TVH: adjusted PBT GS minus 26.3 percent in FY2025, then +17 percent in H1 2026 (20 percent above consensus).
  • Automotive: deliveries minus 13 percent (FY2025) and minus 8.3 percent (H1 2026); margin 5.1 percent (FY2024), 4.7 percent (FY2025), 2.1 percent (H1 2026); EUR 47.2m goodwill impairment; restructuring of 344 roles in consultation. Comparisons ease from FY2027.
  • The risk to the turn: Belron's H2 2026 comparison is demanding (H2 2025 operating profit +20 percent), and its volume recovery depends on the US consumer.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C-5714th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. At the operating level the profit-weighted tailwinds outweigh the headwinds, perhaps 60 to 40; at the level of the share price the headwinds dominate for now. The external picture is therefore "good business weather, bad market weather": the forces acting on Belron's and PHE's earnings are favourable and durable, the forces acting on Automotive are unfavourable and structural but small in profit, and the forces acting on the valuation (rates, risk-off, sector de-rating of car retail, currency) are unfavourable and cyclical.

Driver by driver.

  • Vehicle complexity and ADAS calibration (value chain; Belron): strong tailwind, accelerating, high visibility to about 2028 to 2030; long-dated threat from self-calibration.
  • Ageing parc and independent-aftermarket share gain (industry; PHE): moderate tailwind, stable, high visibility; long-run electric-vehicle drag (maintenance 60 to 70 percent lower).
  • US claims-avoidance cycle (value chain; Belron volume): tailwind now as avoidance eases, but cyclical and exposed to a weakening US consumer.
  • Insurer pricing regulation (value chain; Belron price): forming headwind; probable at the margin, not yet in the numbers.
  • Belgian new-car decline, Chinese entrants, electrification, fiscal changes (industry and sector; Automotive): strong headwind, structural, company-confirmed; Automotive was about 17 percent of FY2025 operating profit and about 6 percent of profit on the H1 2026 run-rate.
  • Industrial cycle (TVH): mild tailwind, recovering.
  • Currency and rates (macro, company-specific transmission): moderate headwind to reported earnings; euro expected stronger; Belron debt cost exposed to higher-for-longer rates.
  • Sector rotation and risk-off (equity level): strong headwind to the multiple now; the reversal trigger in the regime note is the US 10-year below 4.5 percent.

Implication for the Consumer. Do not let the share-price weather leak into the operating model: model Belron and PHE on their own durable drivers, Automotive on a lower plateau, and carry currency and refinancing as explicit sensitivities rather than burying them in the base case.

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Improving clearly at Belron and at the holding's financial management, being forced upward at Automotive and TVH after failures, and mixed on people measures. The improvement is real where it matters most (Belron margin, cash conversion, cost of debt) and reactive elsewhere.

Reasoning and evidence.

Risks

B+545th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is moderate and concentrated. The growth engine is accelerating, the downside multiple is bounded by a valuation already near the bottom of its range, and there is no short interest or activist pressure. The risks that matter run through one asset and one channel: Belron's debt cost, its calibration price and, in the long run, the technology that could remove calibration; plus the perception risk that the share price now leans on a listing whose timing others control, in a regime hostile to complex, levered stories. These risks are correlated rather than independent. For the second set, the right treatment is a single stress case (refinancing +100 basis points, dollar minus 5 percent, tax 30 percent, Belron price and mix about 2 percent, Automotive margin about 2 percent), which takes FY2028 EPS roughly 8 to 10 percent below the central path, alongside the base case.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

C+489th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. Five matter; none is likely within 18 months, and the two with the largest combined weight both run through Belron: a credit or refinancing shock on its EUR 8.3bn of junk debt, and a coordinated insurer move on calibration pricing. Self-calibration is the largest in magnitude but the least plausible inside the horizon. The risks are correlated (a weak US consumer, insurer cost pressure and a rate shock tend to arrive together), so the Consumer should model them as one stress case, not as independent sensitivities.

The risks.

  • 1. Belron refinancing or rate shock. Plausibility low-to-medium (US 10-year near 5.1 percent; the Fed hiking; credit cracking in other leveraged themes). Size: +100 basis points on the stack is about minus EUR 0.6 of EPS (3.4 percent); combined with a 5 percent weaker dollar (minus 2.0 percent) and a 30 percent tax rate (minus 2.8 percent), FY2028 EPS falls about 8 percent and the prior memo's 18-month modal return drops from about 16 to about 10 percent a year.
  • 2. Coordinated US insurer caps or loss of a major administrator role. Plausibility low for a sudden event, medium for gradual erosion. Size: the Key Question report's case of a 20 percent fall in US calibration price (USD 300 to 240) at close to 100 percent drop-through; on my rough arithmetic a few percent of group EPS directly, but more through the multiple because calibration is the growth story.
  • 3. Rapid OEM adoption of self-calibration. Plausibility low before 2030. Size: removes Belron's main margin-expansion engine (about 6 of 17 points of margin expansion since 2018) and impairs its terminal value and listing multiple (78 percent of net asset value).
  • 4. Volkswagen strategic change toward agency or direct distribution in Belgium, or materially worse terms. Plausibility low (78-year contract; Volkswagen is restructuring). Size: Automotive is about 6 percent of profit on the H1 2026 run-rate, but impairments, restructuring cash and D'Ieteren Immo's lease income would follow.
  • 5. Cyber-attack on a logistics-dependent subsidiary. Plausibility low-to-medium (TVH 2023 precedent). Size: TVH lost share permanently; on Belron or PHE the cost would be larger.
  • Lower-order: a euro move to 1.25 (minus about 6 percent of EPS); a TVH warehouse failure; a Moleskine write-down (non-cash, adjusted out).
  • Note on the risks research's verdict: its competing-hypotheses matrix chose "False Friend" with zero hard inconsistencies; the Wisdom Library entry "The Vaguest Explanation Wins a Fewest-Contradictions Contest" warns that the least specific hypothesis tends to win that test. The operating evidence since (volume, margin, cash, leverage) is not superficial; I do not treat False Friend as the base case.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D4620th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Highly concentrated, and more concentrated than the six-unit perimeter suggests: two variables (Belron's price and mix with its drop-through, and the Automotive margin floor) generate about 70 percent of the FY2026 to FY2028 rise in PBT GS, and Belron alone rises from 54 percent of PBT GS in FY2025 to about 65 percent from FY2026 onward. Against the last four to six years, the sources of change are fewer: FY2020 to FY2025 was shaped by two acquisitions (TVH 2021, PHE 2022), a pandemic, an order-backlog boom and bust at Automotive, Belron's fastest margin phase and a EUR 4.2bn recapitalisation; FY2026 to FY2030 has a stable perimeter, no backlog, Belron's margin at half its earlier pace and finance costs moving from the largest drag to a small tailwind. Concentration cuts both ways for predictability: it is unfavourable in that one customer group (US insurers) and one operating variable (Belron's drop-through) can move the whole path, and favourable in that the concentrated driver is the one with the best disclosure, the longest record and the tightest broker agreement, whereas the diffuse remainder (Automotive, TVH, Moleskine, corporate) is where the earlier sets' errors and the sell-side's disagreements sit, and it is small.

The arithmetic of concentration (PBT GS, derived from the second set's base case).

  • FY2025 to FY2028, plus EUR 310m: Belron operations plus EUR 223m (72 percent), Belron finance relief plus EUR 81m (26 percent), PHE plus EUR 66m (21 percent), TVH plus EUR 17m (5 percent), Corporate, Immo and Moleskine plus EUR 24m (8 percent), Automotive minus EUR 101m (minus 33 percent).
  • FY2026 to FY2028, plus EUR 274m: Belron operations plus EUR 153m (56 percent), PHE plus EUR 42m (15 percent), Automotive plus EUR 38m (14 percent), Belron finance relief plus EUR 31m (11 percent), TVH plus EUR 14m (5 percent), other minus EUR 3m.
  • Inside Belron operations, the split is roughly: volume at 3 percent a year on EUR 7bn at a 40 percent incremental margin, about EUR 85m a year at 100 percent; price and mix at 2.5 to 3 percent with a 50 to 70 percent drop-through, about EUR 100m to 130m a year; less labour and equipment inflation. So price and mix times drop-through is the single largest term, and it is the term insurers act on.

What is different from the last four to six years, and why the difference is itself a source of predictability.

  • No perimeter change. FY2021 to FY2023 revenue growth of 1.3, 40.3 and 69.3 percent on FactSet's consolidated lines was acquisitions; FY2026 to FY2030 is the first five-year span since 2020 with none assumed. Perimeter-driven history is unhelpful for forecasting; its absence makes the forward path more legible.
  • Finance costs change sign. Below-the-line costs went from about EUR 220m (FY2023) to EUR 412m (FY2025) and fall to about EUR 344m (FY2026) and EUR 331m (FY2028). The forward years contain no shock of the 2024 kind unless a post-listing recapitalisation repeats it, which the second set rightly keeps outside the base case.
  • Belron's margin gains halve. About 150 to 180 basis points a year in FY2024 to FY2025 (and 319 basis points in H2 2025) becomes about 80 basis points a year to FY2028 and about 25 after. A forecast that asks for less than the recent delivery is more predictable than one that extrapolates it.
  • Automotive goes from boom-and-bust to a low plateau. Its PBT GS swung from about EUR 212m to 215m (FY2024 to FY2025) to about EUR 73m (FY2026); the forward path asks only for stability near a 400,000-unit market and a 21 percent share.

The sell-side's dispersion confirms where the diffuse risk sits. Bernstein's analysis of the FactSet range (FY2026 EPS EUR 9.81 to 14.48) shows most of it is basis (reported basic EPS EUR 8.72 against adjusted EUR 13.52 at Bernstein; proportionate PBT EUR 995.7m against equity-accounted EUR 828m); the genuine disagreement is on Automotive (Bernstein 1.7 percent FY2028 margin against Deutsche Bank near the 4 percent ambition) and TVH (Jefferies 5 percent below consensus for FY2026), while Belron estimates cluster within 1.1 percent of consensus (Bernstein EUR 624.8m against EUR 631.9m for FY2026 PBT GS). The concentrated driver is agreed; the diffuse drivers are disputed and small.

The concentration the Consumer should carry into topic 9 of the memo. One asset (Belron, about 65 percent of PBT GS and about 77 percent of net asset value), one customer type (insurers, 70 to 77 percent of Belron's revenue; 40 percent of North American jobs, 80 percent in the eurozone), one benchmark (NAGS), one capital structure (EUR 8.3bn of junk-rated, 77 percent floating debt) and one country-market for the second driver (Belgium, 400,000 units). Each is monitorable (D-05). The offset is that the concentration is in the business with the least cyclical demand in the group: a cracked windscreen is a safety defect, and Belron's volume fell 13 percent in 2020 while its EBITDA margin stayed above 10 percent.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D4821st of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The change is large and concentrated in the last twelve months: a new group chief executive from private equity and infrastructure arrives in December 2026, the Chief Investment Officer has left and not been replaced, the holding's decision-making was merged into a six-member Operating Committee, two subsidiaries changed their chief executives after underperformance, and the family consolidated control into one branch while the other sells. The combined signal is a shift from an "owner-allocator holding" phase to a "monetise and crystallise" phase, centred on Belron. It is positive for the probability of a Belron liquidity event and negative for near-term continuity and predictability.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Group chief executive. Francis Deprez (CEO since July 2019, ten years at the group; leadership research grade B+; 22.0 percent annualised total shareholder return over ten years; about 500 percent share outperformance since 2019 per ING) hands over to Eric Machiels, a managing director at OMERS infrastructure, in December 2026. The appointment was announced on 9 September 2026, the same day as a 51 percent Automotive profit miss and the opening of the Belron review; the board approved unanimously and Deprez stays to year-end. Two readings: a planned hand-over to a capital-markets specialist for the crystallisation phase, or a reaction to internal dissatisfaction. The files cannot settle it. An infrastructure investor's instincts (long-duration, levered, yield-oriented assets) also fit a "hold and lever Belron" path as much as a "list and narrow the discount" path, so the mandate is inferred, not stated.
  • Finance and investment. CFO Edouard Janssen (since September 2023; ex Solvay, Morgan Stanley) stays; he instituted cash planning and deleveraging and bought EUR 800k of shares in November 2025. CIO Nicolas Saillez left at end-2025; three Investment Directors report directly to the CEO; the Executive Committee and Investment Directors merged into an Operating Committee in March 2026. Investment oversight is concentrated on a CEO who is about to change.
  • Subsidiaries. Belron: Carlos Brito (ex AB InBev CEO) since 2023, stable and strong. TVH: CEO Dominiek Valcke left in August 2025 after missed targets; Patrick Lecluyse is Executive Chairman, with a new CFO, a new COO (Tanja Dysli, ex IKEA) and an industrial CEO. Moleskine: CEO replaced after one year; co-CEOs from February 2026, one of them a D'Ieteren Investment Director.
  • Key actors. The 2024 reorganisation: Nayarit (Nicolas D'Ieteren's branch) bought SPDG's 16.7 percent, reaching 50.1 percent of capital (49.54 percent on later figures) and 53.84 percent of votes; three directors resigned; SPDG sells its remaining 10.6 percent over five years. Belron's private-equity co-owners (about 40 percent) are now the active force for a liquidity event.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C-6411th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The strategy has changed materially in one place and is being reset in another. At Belron, the stance moved from "a happy majority shareholder, and have been for quite a while" to a formal evaluation of liquidity options for the minority stakes, with Rothschild appointed and an Amsterdam listing named; at Automotive, from volume and share to margin protection and "Building Seamless Mobility". The Belron change is resourced in the way that matters (leverage cut to 4.3x) but articulated only as a review: timing, structure and the use of any value released are not stated, and the incoming CEO has not yet set out his priorities.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron. A review, not a decision. D'Ieteren keeps 50.3 percent, so a listing of the minorities' stakes gives D'Ieteren a market price for its main asset but no cash; the proceeds go to the private-equity sellers. The benefit to D'Ieteren's shareholders runs through the holding discount (37 to 45 percent) and through optionality later (a listed Belron can pay dividends, be used as acquisition currency or be partially sold).
  • Automotive. Proposed transformation plan affecting 344 roles (10 to 15 percent of the workforce; 13 percent in one source), site closures, cuts to marketing and consulting spend; savings and timing subject to consultation. Ambition reduced to a margin floor; management lets share slide rather than buy volume.   • Portfolio. PHE has become a pan-European consolidator (Spain, Ireland); TVH runs the Innovatis transformation; Moleskine is under a "fewer, bigger, better" reset and is widely seen as a disposal candidate.
  • The constant. The investment model is unchanged: market-leading platforms in large fragmented markets, the twice-yearly "natural owner" review on seven dimensions, conservative leverage at the holding (loan-to-value under 10 percent) and ring-fenced leverage in subsidiaries (target under 3x for most).

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

B+604th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Real at Belron, nominal at PHE, negative at Automotive and weak at TVH; profit-weighted, real. The important nuance for the model is that Belron's "price and mix" is mostly mix (more calibration content per job), not like-for-like price, and the like-for-like price is under growing insurer pressure; mix should be modelled from penetration, price at about inflation or a little below.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron. Price and mix +5.1 percent in FY2025 with flat volume; +3.4 percent in H1 2026 as volume returned (+3.9 percent). Glass inflation of 10 to 20 percent passed through by semi-annual NAGS-indexed clauses; Safelite holds a 25 percent premium in US cash-pay. Against: insurers argue calibration is labour-intensive and its price should track wage inflation (Cary Group pricing manager); European calibration about EUR 150 against USD 250 to 700 in the US; caps on subsequent calibrations at State Farm and GEICO; experts expect US calibration margins of 80 to 90 percent to decline. The deceleration from 5.1 to 3.4 percent may be partly the arithmetic of volume returning (lower-ticket jobs) rather than price weakness; the files do not separate these.
  • PHE. European parts prices +1.5 percent quarter on quarter in Q2 2026 after a 3.7 percent decline in 2025; PHE's margin held at 9.1 percent and rose to 9.6 percent: pass-through, not real pricing power.
  • TVH. "Pricing power remained highly constrained" in the Americas in FY2025.
  • Automotive. Adverse price and mix in H1 2026; distribution margins tightened; manufacturers pass pricing pressure down to distributors.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C-5613th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. A modest step-up at subsidiary level (PHE, TVH) and a pause at group level while the 2024 recapitalisation is unwound. The deals are strategically sensible and priced with discipline; the integration record is good at PHE and untested in Iberia; the open question is whether a new private-equity-trained CEO, with a deleveraged Belron and a possible listing, steps up group-level M&A, which the risks research names as a capital-misallocation risk for minorities.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Acquisition cash: EUR 60.4m (FY2023), EUR 122m (FY2024), EUR 80.5m (FY2025), EUR 156m (H1 2026) on FactSet's consolidated cash-flow lines.
  • Group-level history: TVH (40 percent, 2021) and PHE (2022, 9.3x EV/EBIT) were funded by capital reductions at Belron; Moleskine (2016) is the weak deal.
  • Discipline markers: bolt-ons at "low-risk, synergetic" scale; larger deals subject to scenario analysis; subsidiary leverage tailored to cash generation.
  • Risks: PHE net debt EUR 1,394m; 51 percent Spanish stakes; the investment team is thinner (no CIO); the new CEO's pipeline unknown. The prior memo set "a capital-markets day led by an acquisition pipeline" as an alarm.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D5216th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The big balance-sheet use already happened (the 2024 recapitalisation, a EUR 4.3bn Belron dividend of which D'Ieteren received about EUR 2.2bn, funding a EUR 74 per share special dividend), and the current phase is the reverse: deleveraging, with a small buyback and a flat ordinary dividend. Shareholder returns are steady rather than stepping up; the step-change option (Belron dividends or a second recapitalisation after listing, or larger buybacks funded by Belron cash) exists but is not mooted.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Buyback: EUR 100m programme launched 22 December 2025, 337,711 shares bought by 15 September 2026 (about EUR 58m), treasury shares 2.4 percent of capital in July 2026; earlier EUR 109m over three years. At least half of any buyback outperformance goes to the family's philanthropic foundations ("solidarity buyback"), an unusual feature that slightly dilutes the benefit to shareholders.
  • Dividend: EUR 2.00 for FY2025, flat; consensus EUR 2.10, 2.40 and 2.73 for FY2026 to FY2028 (payout about 16 percent of adjusted EPS); yield about 1.2 percent at EUR 171.
  • Balance sheet: corporate net financial debt EUR 299.8m at June 2026 (EUR 486.6m excluding inter-segment loans); Automotive paid a EUR 400m dividend to Corporate in FY2025, taking its own net debt from EUR 11.9m to EUR 259.5m (0.8x): the holding is using subsidiary balance sheets to fund the centre. Loan-to-value kept under 10 percent.
  • Belron: 4.3x now, objective under 4x for a listing and under 3x by about 2029; at under 3x Belron could resume distributions, which is the mechanism by which Belron's earnings become D'Ieteren cash.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–58

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Four, each modest alone: Belron's capital structure becoming listing-ready (and re-rating its own debt cost), the crystallisation of hidden assets (D'Ieteren Immo, a Moleskine exit), incentive alignment spreading from Belron to other subsidiaries, and the structural improvement in disclosure that a listed Belron would force. Together they make the holding simpler and more transparent over 18 to 36 months, which is what the discount is mostly about.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron below 4x, then 3x: possible ratings upgrades from junk, cheaper refinancing (repricings already done in 2025 and 2026), and a public-market cost of equity.
  • D'Ieteren Immo EUR 411m of fair value against a lower book value; Moleskine's exit would remove a loss-maker and a management-time drain (ING).
  • Belron's management incentive structures (equity-based LTIPs) are the blueprint for the portfolio; ING asks whether they can be replicated at TVH.
  • A listed Belron would publish quarterly disclosure and an independent market price, removing the "minimal disclosure" component of the discount cited by Bernstein and Deutsche Bank.
  • Insider signal: the CFO's EUR 800k open-market purchase (November 2025) in a group with no shareholding requirements.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

B566th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The scope for change is high at the level of Belron's ownership and low at the level of D'Ieteren's own control and capital allocation. Belron's private-equity co-owners have explicitly forced a process; the D'Ieteren family has explicitly signalled permanence ("cement and anchor the family for decades to come") and has said it will keep 50.3 percent of Belron; no activist exists or can exist with 53.84 percent of votes in one family holding.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The insight for the investment case: the holding discount is an agency discount created by concentrated family control, and the Wisdom Library's pattern ("The Value Gap Plus Low Insider Ownership") says such a discount closes only when a party is able to force it. At D'Ieteren the forcing party sits one level down, at Belron, and its interest is its own exit, not D'Ieteren's discount. A listing therefore addresses the price-discovery and disclosure parts of the discount but not the governance part (dual-class votes, family-first transactions, no minority influence).
  • Family: Nayarit 49.54 percent of capital and 53.84 percent of votes through 5,000,000 participating shares; Chairman Nicolas D'Ieteren, seventh generation.
  • SPDG: exiting about 10 percent over five years; no strategic voice; supplies stock to the buyback.
  • Short interest below the 0.5 percent disclosure threshold; no activist history in a decade (Sofina, with comparable family control, faced two).
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6120th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Broad and real, moderately strong, and adequately resourced, but with the largest change still at the stage of a choice announced rather than an action taken. At least four of the internal forces are strong (a chief-executive change; a strategy and structure change at the main asset; a standards programme at Belron with a restructuring at Automotive; real price and mix gains at Belron) and three more are present (acquisitions, new geographies, a buyback). What holds the rating below the top is that guidance has not been raised, the perimeter is not shrinking, the listing would not put cash into D'Ieteren, and the leadership transition coincides with the most important decision the group faces.

Reasoning and evidence. The resourcing is credible where it matters: Belron's deleveraging (5.3x to 4.3x in a year) is what makes the review feasible, the CFO and Belron's CEO stay, and the Automotive plan is costed. The weaknesses are a thinned investment team (no CIO), an unproven new CEO and a family whose stated objective is permanence, which limits how radical the changes can become. For the model, the corporate changes affect FY2027 to FY2028 earnings mostly through Belron's finance costs, the Automotive cost base and PHE's Spanish contribution, and affect the multiple mostly through the Belron event.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C+557th of 30

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. A real staircase exists, but only one step is genuinely novel and large: the Belron liquidity event, most plausibly in months 6 to 18. The near steps are mostly transition events (a new chief executive, a restructuring settlement, FY2026 results with new guidance) that carry as much risk of disappointment as of surprise; the far steps are the FY2028 targets, already known. The staircase is well-founded in direction (a formal process, motivated sellers, a listing-ready balance sheet) and weakly founded in timing (market windows and a risk-off regime, sellers who control the clock, a new CEO whose mandate is unstated).

Within six months (to April 2027).

  • Eric Machiels takes office (December 2026): novel, outcome unknown; positive if the mandate is crystallisation and buybacks, negative if acquisitions.
  • News from the Belron review (adviser mandates, preparatory steps, any private-equity stake sale): novel, well-founded on process, timing uncertain.
  • Conclusion of the Automotive consultation, with quantified savings: moderately novel, well-founded.
  • FY2026 results and FY2027 guidance (10 March 2027): FY2026 likely at or above consensus; FY2027 guidance at risk of a below-consensus framing; possible write-downs (Moleskine).
  • Completion of the EUR 100m buyback and possibly a new programme: low novelty.
  • Monthly Belgian registrations (Febiac), first evidence of an Automotive floor.

Months six to eighteen (May 2027 to April 2028).

  • The Belron listing (Amsterdam mooted, EUR 30 to 40bn) or another liquidity event: highly novel, the decisive step; probability meaningful but not certain (the prior memo put about 60 percent by March 2028).
  • The new chief executive's strategy statement or capital-markets day.
  • H1 2027 results (September 2027): Belron's margin toward 24 to 25 percent, penetration toward the mid-50s, leverage below 4x, possible credit-rating upgrades.
  • First full year of PHE's Spanish assets; a Moleskine exit; a decision on surplus D'Ieteren Immo property.

Months eighteen to thirty-six (2028 to 2029).

  • FY2028 targets (Belron above 25 percent, TVH 16 percent, Automotive about 4 percent, Moleskine 15 percent): known, low novelty.
  • Belron below 3x leverage and a Belron dividend policy (the channel from Belron's earnings to D'Ieteren's cash): novel if it comes, and it is the step that would most narrow the holding discount.
  • Completion of SPDG's sell-down (removing the overhang); post-listing lock-up expiries and index inclusion for Belron.
  • Clarity on self-calibration timelines (a negative step if manufacturers commit).
2c

Net internal change forces negative/positive

F5424th of 302 answers
Judgement58C-2160
Group judgement

Internal change forces

F5424th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The internal change picture is broad and meaningful but not yet decisive. The holding is moving from a phase of building and levering (2019 to 2024: TVH, PHE, Belron's margin transformation, the family recapitalisation) to a phase of deleveraging and crystallising (2025 onward: bridge repaid, Belron at 4.3x, a formal Belron review, a buyback, a costed Automotive restructuring), with a new chief executive whose background fits the second phase. The changes are well-resourced financially but thin in people (no CIO, churn at TVH and Moleskine), they leave the perimeter wide, and they have not translated into raised guidance. The most important single fact for the Consumer is that the change that could move the share price most (the Belron listing) does not change D'Ieteren's earnings or cash in itself, whereas the changes that move earnings (Belron deleveraging, the Automotive cost reset, PHE Spain) are incremental and largely in consensus. The positive skew in this group is therefore mostly in the multiple; the negative skew (an acquisitive new CEO, a kitchen-sink rebase in March 2027) is mostly in earnings and capital allocation.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

B725th of 30

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

B725th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The value chain that carries D'Ieteren's value is one of the best-structured in the European services universe: a dominant global leader (about 17 to 19 percent share, more than twice number two) over a fragmented tail, a technical barrier that rises with every model year, demand mandated by safety regulation and paid largely by insurers, and competitors that kept prices up when volumes fell. Share consolidates toward the operator with calibration capability and claims integration, which is Belron. The fragility of that structure is concentrated in one actor, the insurer, which is both the route to market and the concentrated buyer and whose attitude to calibration pricing has hardened since 2024, and in one long-dated technology, self-calibration. PHE's chain is fragmented and favourable but exposed to low-cost challengers; Automotive's chain has turned hostile (sole supplier under strain, Chinese entrants, fiscal change, falling share) and is the source of every current red flag. The historical patterns of the sector explain the share-price divergence: the market pays for non-manufacturer aftermarket and punishes car retail, and D'Ieteren is priced with the latter until a catalyst separates the two.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?C+6510th of 30full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

B666th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The intermediary is the heart of Belron's moat and also its main fragility. Insurers and claims administrators route about 70 percent of claims to Belron and have just deepened that dependence (State Farm); the same insurers are concentrated buyers who renegotiate semi-annually and are starting to regulate calibration price. Net, the route to market helps strongly today; it is fragile to a change in insurer policy, not to competitors.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Helping: Safelite administers State Farm's National Glass Program from July 2025 (replacing LYNX), with software that steers unaligned claims into Safelite's network; Travelers' largest preferred vendor; Liberty Mutual uses Safelite's scheduling platform; insurers need national coverage that regionals lack.
  • Hindering at the margin: Belgian leasing companies favour Autoglass Clinic (over 50 percent of that channel); sceptics say insurers may look for alternatives to Safelite as administrator.
  • Automotive: a dealer network being thinned; Volkswagen D'Ieteren Finance (joint venture) finances about 30 percent of sales; fleet lessors (Ayvens and peers) are under pressure, which weakens a key channel.
  • PHE: over 30 percent of B2B orders on its own platforms (Autossimo, Truckissimo); about 7,000 affiliated garages under the AD banner: supportive and stable.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

A702nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Competitor behaviour helps Belron's relative position (the main US competitors are weaker, less profitable and growing more slowly), hurts Automotive (discounting in a shrinking market), and is neutral to PHE. Belron's position against existing competitors is not fragile in the US and moderately fragile in parts of Europe.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Boyd: volume minus 0 to 2 percent in early 2026, same-store sales minus 1 percent, margin structurally lower (12 percent EBITDA margin in one source, 18.3 percent operating-income margin in another); responds by bundling collision, glass and calibration.
  • Driven Brands' Auto Glass Now: same-store sales +7.2 percent in Q1 2026 but a 4.3 percent margin; Driven has had accounting delays.
  • Cary Group: European expansion with private-equity backing.
  • Belgian car competitors: discounting; Chinese entrants targeting fleets.
  • PHE's peers: LKQ Europe shrinking; Inter Cars and Auto Partner gaining with lower cost ratios and gross margins of about 29 to 30 percent helped by parts inflation.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

B+583rd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Disruption is acting on demand (more technology per vehicle: favourable to Belron), on competitors (calibration and mobile service: favourable to scale), on regulators (mandates: favourable; billing rules: unfavourable), on suppliers (manufacturers and Tier 1s developing self-calibration: the long-dated threat) and on the car-distribution chain (Chinese entrants, agency, electrification: unfavourable to Automotive). Near-term fragility is low for Belron and high for Automotive.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Demand: forward-collision warning and emergency braking approach 100 percent of new US vehicles by 2030; parc renewal lifts the camera-equipped share each year.
  • Competitors: one-person shops sublet calibration or exit.
  • Regulators and insurers: billing scrutiny grows with ticket size.
  • Suppliers: Aptiv, Mobileye, Bosch, Hyundai, Honda, Stoneridge working on self-correction; Fuyao integrating lidar and cameras into the glass, which raises complexity and makes self-calibration both more desirable for manufacturers and harder to achieve after a glass swap.
  • Insurers: AI and data in claims favour the administrator (Safelite) that owns the routing software.
  • Car chain: Volkswagen restructuring, Chinese plug-in hybrids untariffed, BYD insourcing distribution.
