C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

A883rd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Number one in each of its three profit-carrying activities, and by a wide margin in the one that carries the value: Belron is the global number one in vehicle glass repair, replacement and recalibration with about 17 to 19 percent share, more than double the next competitor; TVH is the European number one in forklift spare parts with more than 50 percent; PHE is number one in French independent parts distribution and top three in Western Europe. D'Ieteren Automotive is Belgium's leading importer but losing share.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron: 17 percent of a EUR 40bn global market (Bernstein; "more than double its next three competitors combined") or 19 percent in FY2025 against 15 percent in 2015 (earnings review), against Boyd Group at 7 to 8 percent, Driven Brands 5 percent, Cary Group 1 percent; in the US, Safelite Solutions administers 65 to 75 percent of non-collision glass claims. Belron operates in 42 countries.
  • TVH: over 50 percent of European forklift aftermarket parts; global independent leader; Kramp is larger in European agricultural parts.
  • PHE: number one independent distributor in France (62 percent of its sales), top three in Western Europe, in a market where the top seven hold only 25 percent.
  • D'Ieteren Automotive: 21.5 to 21.6 percent of Belgian new-car registrations in H1 2026 (23.0 to 23.3 percent in FY2025; 24.0 percent in FY2024); 28.8 percent of the Belgian battery-electric market in 2025.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C-6613th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In global glass the top players sit right at the 30 percent threshold with a very steep curve at the top (17 against 7, 5 and 1) and a 70 percent tail of independents; in the US insured channel the structure is far more concentrated through claims administration. European parts distribution is fragmented (top seven 25 percent), which is the roll-up runway rather than a structural support. The structure that carries the value is "dominant leader over a fragmented tail", the most favourable shape for share gain.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Named top four in glass about 30 percent (17 + 7 + 5 + 1); the leader is more than twice number two; the tail is one-person shops that cannot fund calibration equipment.
  • US: Safelite's 65 to 75 percent of non-collision claims administration gives it effective control of claim routing, far above its share of physical capacity.
  • Europe: country-by-country structure; smaller operators "frequently secure large insurance agreements" (Cary Group), so European concentration is lower and pricing tougher.
  • Buyer concentration is the counterweight: the top three insurers hold 40 to 75 percent of each European market; State Farm about 17 to 19 percent of US auto insurance.
  • Parts: top seven 25 percent, about 50,000 small distributors; low-cost Central European players gaining.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C+669th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Belron's share is stable to rising (about 15 to 19 percent over ten years) and new entrants have taken little; the share loss in the group is at Automotive, where Chinese manufacturers took about five points of European share in a single year, and at TVH after its 2023 cyber-attack, where some customers permanently dual-sourced.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron: +4 points of global share since 2015; organic growth 7.3 percent against Boyd's minus 1 percent same-store sales in H1 2026; State Farm administration from July 2025 is a multi-year volume tailwind.
  • Entrants in glass: Cary Group at 1 percent globally but over 50 percent of the Belgian leasing channel and doubling its Belgian footprint; NWG (Australia) upgraded ADAS capability in 2025; no entrant has taken meaningful global share.
  • Automotive: share 24.0 percent (FY2024), about 23 percent (FY2025), about 21.5 percent (H1 2026); Chinese manufacturers' European share 13 percent in August 2026 against under 8 percent a year earlier; Chinese brands hold 11 percent of European sales on another measure.
  • TVH: the 2023 cyber-attack led customers to diversify suppliers, which the risks research says caps volume recovery.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

A802nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In glass, yes, and the largest and the most advantaged are the same company: calibration capability and insurer integration are the advantages, and both require scale, so consolidation runs toward Belron mechanically. In parts distribution, share is consolidating toward the most advantaged (low-cost, double-digit-growth Central European players) rather than the largest (LKQ shrinks); PHE sits in between, outgrowing the incumbents but not the low-cost leaders. In Belgian car distribution, share moves away from the incumbent single-manufacturer importer.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Glass: calibration machinery that independents "cannot afford" (NOVUS Glass director); insurers "expressly prefer large-scale operators" (Bernstein, UK, September 2026); 38 percent of eligible vehicles calibrated industry-wide, meaning most of the market's calibration capacity is still to be built and the scaled operator builds it first.
  • Parts: Inter Cars +12 percent against a sector median near 4 percent; LKQ Europe minus 5.1 percent; MEKO zero; PHE 6.0 percent organic in H1 2026, with margins of 9.1 to 9.6 percent above MEKO (about 6.5 percent) and Inter Cars (5.8 percent).
  • Cars: consolidation toward manufacturer-direct and Chinese entrants (BYD's Benelux insourcing; Leapmotor leases at EUR 49 a month).
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C+749th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In the arena that carries the value, US insured glass, competitors and the leader are margin-first and prices did not fall when volume fell; in European glass the attitude is more price-competitive but rational; in Belgian new cars it is volume-first with willingness to suffer losses and prices fall with volume. Profit-weighted, the attitude is benign.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Test of attitude: US auto-glass unit volumes fell 5 to 6 percent in 2024, yet Belron's price and mix rose 5.1 percent in FY2025: prices did not fall with volume.
  • US: contracts reviewed semi-annually on service metrics; the leader commands "a modest pricing premium justified by scale"; regional cash-pay competitors price-match, but the cash channel is about 30 percent of North American revenue.
  • Europe: prices "aligned tightly to maintain volume"; calibration price benchmarked to wage inflation; Cary's buyout at 22x EV/EBIT shows private-equity capital willing to fund expansion.
  • Belgian cars: heavy discounting to clear stock; Chinese entrants subsidise entry; distribution margins tighter; prices fell with volume.
  • Parts: a Polish price war is the named risk as inflation cools; prices fell 3.7 percent in 2025.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D5417th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Among competitors the attitude in glass has been stable through a claims trough; the attitude that has changed is the buyer's. Since the 2022 to 2024 premium inflation, US insurers have moved from passive payers to active regulators of calibration billing, and that change is the one most likely to compress Belron's price over three years. A shark attack by a glass competitor is unlikely in the US insured channel (Safelite controls routing) and more plausible in European pockets; in Belgian cars it is already happening.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Insurers: Progressive pricing policies; State Farm and GEICO caps on subsequent calibrations; USAA pre- and post-scan reports; experts expect US calibration margins to decline as audit awareness rises. Bernstein's counter: insurers can pass higher claim costs into premiums, which limits their resistance to non-discretionary calibration.
  • Competitors: Boyd's bundling is a slow, contractual form of competition; Cary's Belgian expansion is local; no evidence of below-cost pricing in glass.
  • Belgian cars: Chinese entrants are the "O'Leary" actors (situation-dependent, subsidised, tariff-window opportunism); Volkswagen itself is discounting the ID range by up to EUR 6,000.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

B725th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Supportive and only moderately fragile where the value is; unsupportive and fragile where the value is not. Belron's structure (dominant leader, fragmented tail, technical barrier rising, regulation-driven demand, rational competitors) is close to the best an industry can offer; its fragility lies on the other side of the table (concentrated insurers starting to regulate price) and at the far end of the time horizon (self-calibration). PHE's structure is moderately supportive (fragmented, ageing parc) with a low-cost-entrant fragility. Automotive's structure has turned hostile.

Reasoning and evidence. Belron is about 57 percent of group-share operating profit and about three-quarters of value; PHE about 18 percent of FY2025 operating profit at group share (derived; 9.1 percent margin on EUR 2,937.1m x 91 percent is about EUR 243m); TVH about 7 percent (derived: 13.4 percent on EUR 1,668.5m x 40 percent is about EUR 89m); Automotive about 17 percent (FY2025, falling to about 6 percent on the H1 2026 run-rate). Weighting each structure by profit gives a clearly supportive whole, with the fragility concentrated in one named, monitorable variable: the US insurer's attitude to calibration pricing.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C-5811th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Substitutes are a rising threat to Automotive and a slowly rising, long-dated threat to Belron's calibration revenue. For glass replacement itself there is no substitute; for the insured route there is a partial one (cash repair at an independent, or deferral) that grows when insurance becomes expensive.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Glass: repair instead of replacement (Belron does both); deferral and cash repair when deductibles reach USD 1,000; self-calibration as a substitute for the recalibration service.
  • Cars: mobility budgets from 2027, car-sharing (Poppy is D'Ieteren's own), used cars, manufacturer-direct leasing.
  • Parts: electric vehicles need 60 to 70 percent less maintenance (sector note) or about 40 percent fewer interventions (other sources); combustion parts expected robust for at least a decade.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

B+684th of 30

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

A762nd of 308 answers
Judgement70C-4175C-4275C-4362C-4474C-4566C-4645C-4770
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

A762nd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

D'Ieteren's competitive advantage is strong, evidenced by behaviour rather than assertion, and mostly widening, but it sits almost entirely in one asset. Belron combines customer integration (insurer claims systems, the State Farm administrator role), production advantage (proprietary supply chain, mobile calibration), purchasing scale and a regulation-driven technical barrier, and converts them into top-decile outputs (23 percent margin, 89 percent cash conversion, Net Promoter Score 87, growth well above competitors). The mechanics compound with time. Its pricing power is applied, not conjectured, but it has been used to retain scale economies rather than share them, which sets up the main medium-term balancing force: insurer pushback on calibration price. PHE and TVH add narrower but real moats; Automotive adds none. For the model, the strength of the advantage justifies forecasting Belron's share gains and margin progress with confidence through FY2028; the concentration of the advantage means the group's earnings quality and multiple depend on one variable (Belron's price and mix) more than the six-unit perimeter suggests.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

A772nd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Belron holds strong, behaviourally evidenced advantages in four of the five categories, and three of them are widening; PHE and TVH hold moderate, narrower moats (logistics density and catalogue breadth); D'Ieteren Automotive holds a contractual franchise, not an advantage, and it is narrowing. Because Belron is about three-quarters of value, the group's moat is strong but concentrated in one asset.

Belron, category by category.

  • Customer-related (strong, widening). Integration into insurers' claims systems; Safelite administers State Farm's national glass programme and 65 to 75 percent of US non-collision claims; decade-plus carrier relationships renewed on service metrics; Net Promoter Score 87.0 (record, 2025). The State Farm mandate widened the moat in July 2025.
  • Production-related (strong, widening). A proprietary supply chain (Bilzen European distribution centre with over 500,000 windscreens; about 3,000 service centres; mobile vans; central sourcing of glass, adhesives and calibration kit) that independents relying on wholesalers cannot match; mobile static recalibration (US 2025, UK 2026) widens it further.
  • Scale-based (strong, stable). Largest or second-largest global glass buyer (about 10 million units in 2024); domestic US sourcing shields Safelite from tariffs that hurt import-dependent rivals.
  • Regulatory or political (moderate, widening). Not a licence, but camera mandates turn a glass job into a technical service only scaled operators can do to standard; Belron lobbies for repair-information rights.
  • Culture and knowledge (moderate to strong, stable). Technician training and calibration know-how at scale; engagement 87.1 percent.

Other businesses. PHE: logistical density (H+2 delivery for 90 percent of SKUs from over 400 automated sites), digital ordering platforms (over 30 percent of B2B orders), AD buying-group rebates, private label about 15 percent of mix: moderate, stable. TVH: over 1 million SKUs (930,000 in stock), 44 million machine-part references, 95 percent same-day shipment, over 50 percent European forklift-parts share: strong in its niche, dented by the 2023 cyber-attack. D'Ieteren Automotive: an exclusive 78-year Volkswagen contract that protects against other distributors but not against falling demand, Chinese brands or the manufacturer's own terms: narrowing.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A842nd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Belron's scale is more than twice its nearest competitor's and confers the four things that matter in this industry: purchasing terms on glass, national coverage that insurers require, the capital to equip every site and van for calibration, and the claims-routing software position. Scale here is the advantage itself, not a by-product.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron revenue EUR 6,722m (FY2025, 100 percent); share 17 to 19 percent against Boyd 7 to 8 percent, Driven 5 percent, Cary 1 percent.
  • Margin outcome of the scale: Belron 22.9 to 23.0 percent operating margin against Boyd (structurally lower) and Driven's 4.3 percent; peer EBITA margins of about 8 to 12 percent (Boyd, Cary) per Bernstein.
  • Insurers' stated reason for preferring Safelite: "unmatched national scale" (Travelers, April 2026); emerging players "lack the geographic coverage and scale" (Liberty Mutual).
  • At the holding: a 27-person head office running six businesses with combined group-share revenue of EUR 12.1bn: scale does not confer synergies across the businesses (none are claimed), only a lean centre.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

A783rd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Inferiority is clearly disproved at Belron and at PHE, and Belron's utility is plausibly leading for both its customers (the insurer and the driver). The evidence is behavioural: insurers hand Belron their claims administration and keep it as preferred vendor during a cost squeeze, drivers pay a premium in the cash channel, and Belron grows faster than its competitors.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • For the insurer: digitised claims, national coverage, consistent quality, faster claim cycles from mobile calibration (Travelers calls mobile capability a key differentiator), and lower total claims cost through routing.
  • For the driver: same-day mobile repair at home, calibrated to standard (failure leaves safety systems disabled and creates liability); Net Promoter Score 87.0.
  • Relative evidence: Belron organic growth 7.3 percent against Boyd's minus 1 percent same-store sales; Auto Glass Now's 7.2 percent "trailing Belron's North American growth"; Safelite's 25 percent premium retained in cash-pay.
  • PHE: H+2 delivery and first-time-fix part identification; organic growth above market and above LKQ.
  • TVH: 95 percent same-day shipment; uptime over price; but the 2023 cyber-attack made some customers dual-source.
  • Automotive: utility is Volkswagen's product, now weaker against Chinese value offers.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

A741st of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Belron has applied pricing power, demonstrably: 5.1 percent price and mix with flat volume in FY2025, full pass-through of 10 to 20 percent glass inflation, and a retained cash-channel premium, all without losing share. The power is real but now being tested: its highest-margin component (calibration) is the one insurers have started to cap, and European pricing is already structurally lower. Model price and mix at a level below the H1 2026 rate, with mix (penetration) carrying most of it.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Applied: price and mix +5.1 percent (FY2025), +3.4 percent (H1 2026); NAGS-indexed semi-annual clauses; Safelite 25 percent premium; prices held when US volumes fell 5 to 6 percent in 2024.
  • Utility not eroded: share and Net Promoter Score rose over the same period; insurers stayed.
  • Erosion signals: State Farm and GEICO capping subsequent calibrations; Progressive pricing policies; USAA documentation requirements; European calibration about EUR 150 against USD 250 to 700 in the US; Cary Group says calibration price increases should track wage inflation; experts expect US calibration margins of 80 to 90 percent to decline as audit awareness rises.
  • PHE: pass-through of parts inflation (nominal, not real). TVH: constrained in 2025. Automotive: none.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D3019th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Little. Belron has retained most of its scale economies as margin (5.4 percent in 2017 to 23.0 percent in 2025), sharing them with insurers only de facto through service quality and lower claims-handling cost. That is good for near-term earnings and is the root of the main medium-term risk: concentrated buyers who see a supplier's margin quadruple tend to claw part of it back, which is what US insurers are now beginning to do on calibration.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • No stated "scale economies shared" value or pricing philosophy in the files.
  • De facto sharing: mobile service, digital booking and technician routing reduce insurers' cycle times and customers' inconvenience.
  • The balancing loop this creates (margin expansion invites buyer pushback) is set out for the second set under feedback loops: the more of calibration's near-100 percent incremental margin Belron keeps, the stronger insurers' incentive to regulate its price.
  • PHE: the AD buying group passes purchasing benefits to affiliated garages, which is sharing in a modest form.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

A742nd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Strongly at Belron and at the group's balance sheet; negatively at Automotive. At Belron each model year adds camera content that raises the equipment, training and data bar, each insurer integration raises switching costs, and each half of cash generation lowers leverage and finance costs. These are mechanical, not heroic. At Automotive the mechanics run the other way: falling volume deleverages a fixed retail cost base and erodes share.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron: penetration +5 to 6 points a year; share +0.4 points a year since 2015; margin up every year cited.
  • Balance sheet: Belron 5.5x (mid-2024) to 4.3x (June 2026); Belron finance costs at group share fell from about EUR 128m in H1 2025 to about EUR 104m in H1 2026 (derived from segment operating result and PBT), helped by repricings and a weaker dollar.
  • Buyback: about 1 percent of shares a year.
  • Automotive: deliveries minus 13 percent and minus 8.3 percent; a 24 percent decremental margin on the H1 2026 sales fall (derived); share minus 1.5 points a year.

Management is a good allocator and a weak-to-average operator outside Belron, inside a governance frame that serves the family first. C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

C-6015th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Moderate at the holding, big at Belron. The new CEO may raise the holding's ambition; the family's objective caps how far.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

A701st of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Smart and, in its big moves, bold: rotating the group from a single-manufacturer Belgian car importer into aftermarket compounders, funding TVH and PHE from Belron capital reductions, and backing calibration at Belron early were the right calls and built a 22 percent annualised ten-year return. The weaknesses are a strategy for Automotive that is now survival, the absence of any synergy logic across the portfolio (which the market prices as a conglomerate discount), and a capital structure choice in 2024 that served the family's reorganisation at the cost of balance-sheet resilience.

PHE bought in 2022 at 9.3x EV/EBIT and compounding; Belron's moat widened through calibration and mobile service; a twice-yearly review of each company on seven dimensions; decentralised operating model; robustness across environments is good at Belron (GDP-agnostic demand) and poor at Automotive (exposed to every cycle); the Wisdom Library's "Linked Businesses Are Not a Conglomerate" applies: with no linkage named, each division should be valued on its own and no synergy credited.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F2829th of 30

  • For minorities: seven of twelve directors independent; an ad hoc committee of independents vetted the 2024 transaction under Article 7:97; KPMG renewed, no restatements; the CFO's EUR 800k purchase; long-term incentives tied to total shareholder return above the MSCI World index; a 22 percent annualised ten-year return for all shareholders; zero activist history.
  • Why it matters for the model: governance is the part of the holding discount (37 to 45 percent against 28 to 32 percent at GBL and Sofina) that a Belron listing does not remove.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Group (company definition) free cash flow: EUR 507.0m (FY2024), EUR 373.9m (FY2025; lower on working-capital normalisation at Automotive and higher interest), EUR 485.1m (H1 2026, against EUR 256.8m).
  • FactSet consolidated (Automotive, PHE, Moleskine): free cash flow 102.7 percent of reported net income in FY2025; FY2024's figure (minus 338 percent) is distorted and should not be used; consensus free cash flow EUR 354m, 424m and 443m against adjusted net income of EUR 557m, 707m and 814m.
  • Working-capital "false friends" flagged by the risks research: Automotive's EUR 362m trading cash flow in FY2024 came from destocking a collapsing order book; PHE used EUR 89.8m of non-recourse factoring; TVH's 70 percent conversion came partly from deferring growth capital expenditure; Belron and Moleskine built inventory in late 2025 ahead of tariffs. Consolidated net working capital rose from 8.0 percent of trailing revenue at end-2025 to 14.3 percent at June 2026 (seasonal plus Spain).
  • TVH holds inventory at 35 to 40 percent (or 43 percent) of sales; PHE about 20 percent.
  • The gap to research: Belron's dividend policy to its shareholders after deleveraging, which determines when Belron's earnings become D'Ieteren cash.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D2021st of 30

The adjusted figures are the right basis for the model because the market and the company use them, but the Consumer should carry a recurring "other adjusting items" charge of about EUR 100m-plus a year in any cash or reported view, and should not treat the adjusted-to-reported gap as shrinking.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Other adjusting items EUR 132m (2025) against EUR 115m (2024), including EUR 44.3m of system-integrator fees (an IT transformation that looks recurring) and EUR 9.8m of legal provisions; purchase-price amortisation from the Belron, PHE and TVH acquisitions is the largest recurring excluded item but is not sized in the files.
  • One-offs: EUR 47.2m goodwill impairment at Automotive (H1 2026); EUR 76.4m Moleskine inter-segment waiver; a EUR 72.6m tax-dispute liability reversed in 2025; cash-settled share-based payment expense swinging from EUR 29.0m (H1 2024) to EUR 4.4m (H1 2025).
  • Tax: reported consolidated rate 43.3 percent (FY2025) and 55.4 percent (FY2024), distorted by equity accounting; adjusted rate about 28 percent, implied and not guided (the business-foundations digest calculates 29.5 percent).
  • Leakage: 49.7 percent of Belron belongs to others (already netted in group share); Spanish minorities (49 percent) inside PHE; PHE's own 9 percent minority; a multi-class share structure (52.6m basic against 58.0m diluted across classes on FactSet) that makes the per-share denominator ambiguous.
  • No restatements; KPMG unqualified.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F3626th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Very material, and the single largest amplifier of both EPS growth and EPS risk. Finance costs absorb about 30 percent of the group-share operating result (about EUR 411m of EUR 1,367m in FY2025) and about a third of Belron's own operating result. Deleveraging is turning this into the largest component of EPS growth; a rate or refinancing shock would reverse it. Comfort is moderate and improving: Belron converts about 89 percent of trading profit to cash, has repriced its loans and is at 4.3x, but it is junk-rated, the holding sits on top of it, and the rate regime has turned against borrowers.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron: net debt EUR 8,342.9m at 4.3x (implied LTM EBITDA about EUR 1.94bn); finance costs EUR 506.2m (FY2025, 100 percent) against an operating result of about EUR 1,546m; at group share about EUR 260m in FY2025, about EUR 104m in H1 2026 (about EUR 128m in H1 2025): a run-rate fall of about EUR 45 to 50m a year at group share.
  • Arithmetic for the model: every EUR 1bn of Belron debt repaid at about 6 percent saves about EUR 60m at 100 percent, about EUR 30m at group share, about EUR 0.42 of EPS (3 percent). A 100 basis-point rise on the whole stack costs about EUR 0.6 of EPS (3.4 percent of FY2028 earnings).
  • Other debt: PHE EUR 1,394m (term loan add-on at Euribor plus 300); TVH EUR 901.4m at 3.2x; Automotive EUR 259.5m; corporate EUR 486.6m excluding inter-segment loans (loan-to-value under 10 percent). FactSet consolidated net debt to EBITDA 3.94x (includes leases, excludes Belron and TVH).
  • Context: Moody's and S&P downgraded Belron to junk after the 2024 recapitalisation; US 10-year near 5.1 percent; the Fitness for Fighting note's question "where is the growth financed, and on whose balance sheet?" applies directly.
  • Gap: Belron's debt maturity profile and fixed-floating mix are not in the files.

5a

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

C4412th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price will contribute less than in the last three years and its margin effect will fade faster than its revenue effect. At Belron, price and mix falls from 5.1 percent (FY2025) and 3.4 percent (H1 2026) to about 3.0 percent (FY2027), 2.5 percent (FY2028) and 2.0 percent (FY2029 to FY2030), and its drop-through falls with it, because the high-margin part (calibration fees, NAGS mark-ups on complex glass) is the part US insurers are now moving to regulate, while the part that is pure pass-through (glass input inflation under NAGS indexation) carries little margin. Elsewhere price is a pass-through (PHE, TVH at plus 1.5 to 2.5 percent a year with no margin gain) or a drag (Automotive at minus 1 to 2 percent in FY2026 to FY2027). The most important refinement to the first set is that calibration is about 8 percent of Belron's revenue, so calibration mix adds under one point of growth a year; most of Belron's "price and mix" is the price of increasingly complex glass, set against the NAGS benchmark, which means the insurer pressure that matters is not only on calibration fees but on the mark-up over NAGS.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • What Belron's price and mix is made of. Calibration penetration 47.2 percent (FY2025) and 52.1 percent (H1 2026); calibration about 8 percent of Belron revenue (Bernstein, September 2026), consistent with about 3.5m calibrations a year at about EUR 150 to 160 each; calibration explains about 30 percent of Belron's recent margin expansion (Bernstein, May 2026). Each five points of penetration therefore adds about 0.8 points of revenue. The remainder of the 3.4 to 5.1 percent comes from glass complexity (head-up display, camera brackets, acoustic and heated glass, Fuyao's fused lidar windscreen) priced off the NAGS list, and from pass-through of glass cost inflation (windscreen costs up about 15 percent in 2025 on tariff anticipation; NAGS updates quarterly, so pass-through lags about three months). The deceleration from 5.1 to 3.4 percent is partly the arithmetic of volume returning (+3.9 percent in H1 2026) to lower-ticket jobs, and partly the end of the 2024 to 2025 glass-inflation pass-through.
  • The balancing force on price (evidence since January 2026). GEICO and State Farm are "leading the industry in reducing calibration reimbursements", and Liberty Mutual is evaluating similar changes within 14 months (former Gerber executive, April 2026); State Farm requires its ADAS Map tool for full reimbursement (late 2025); Travelers is exploring mechanical-rate time studies capping a simple calibration at about one hour at about USD 200 (April 2026), against dynamic USD 250 to 350, static USD 250 to 400 and dual USD 450 to 700. Contracts are renegotiated on rolling two-to-three-year cycles (management, September 2024), so the effect comes through gradually from 2027. European calibration (about EUR 150, priced off labour cost) is the reference for where US prices are heading; Cary Group's European contracts are CPI-indexed at 1 to 2 percent a year.
  • What limits the pushback. No national alternative network exists; subletting calibration adds days to a claim; insurers avoid safety-liability headlines (Nationwide former director, June 2025); Safelite administers State Farm's glass programme from July 2025 (competitors expect Safelite's share of State Farm jobs to rise from 30 to 40 percent toward 70 to 80 percent). So I expect a decline in US calibration price per job of perhaps 10 to 20 percent phased over FY2027 to FY2029 (Travelers-style hourly pricing on simple static work), not a collapse, with volume and penetration still rising.
  • The size of the risk, quantified. A 20 percent cut in US calibration prices (USD 300 to 240, the Key Question report's case) on about EUR 540m of calibration revenue, of which perhaps 60 percent is US, is about EUR 65m of revenue at close to full drop-through: about 3.5 percent of Belron's FY2027 operating result, about 90 basis points of margin, EUR 33m of PBT GS, EUR 0.45 of EPS (about 3 percent). My base case includes about a third of this by FY2028 (inside the 2.5 percent price and mix); the stress case in P-06 includes all of it.
  • Drop-through assumed. Belron price and mix drop-through about 70 percent in FY2026 to FY2027 and about 50 to 55 percent from FY2028, as pass-through (low margin) becomes a larger share of a smaller total. Revenue contribution from price and mix at Belron: about EUR 230m (FY2026), EUR 210m (FY2027), EUR 185m (FY2028).
  • PHE. European parts prices fell 3.7 percent in 2025 and rose 1.5 percent quarter on quarter in Q2 2026 (premium segments 2.6 percent); Inter Cars' and Auto Partner's gross margins rose with them. PHE held 9.1 percent through deflation and reached 9.6 percent in H1 2026: pass-through with share gain, not pricing power. I assume price of about 2 percent in FY2026 and 1.5 to 2 percent after, margin-neutral. The risk is a Polish-led price war as inflation cools (sector digest, 7 October 2026), and LKQ's promotional pricing in the UK and Benelux; the opportunity is LKQ's German ERP failure pushing customers to PHE where they overlap (Benelux).
  • TVH. "Pricing power remained highly constrained" in the Americas in FY2025; availability rather than price drives purchase (95 percent same-day shipment). Assume 1.5 to 2 percent, margin-neutral.
  • Automotive. Adverse price and mix in H1 2026; Volkswagen funding discounts of up to EUR 6,000 on the ID range; Chinese plug-in hybrids untariffed; corporate fleet demand expected to taper from 2027 as the last year of 100 percent deductibility for corporate electric vehicles ends (Jefferies, 15 October 2026). Assume minus 1.5 percent in FY2026, minus 1 percent in FY2027, flat after; this is one reason my Automotive margin stays well below the 4 percent ambition.
  • Group view. Price adds about 2.0 to 2.5 percent a year to group-share revenue in FY2026 to FY2027 and about 1.5 percent a year after, against about 3 to 4 percent in FY2024 to FY2025; its contribution to the group-share margin falls from about 60 to 70 basis points a year to about 30 basis points.

Disagreement with the first set. C-17 said Belron's price and mix "is mostly mix (more calibration content per job)"; on Bernstein's 8 percent revenue share, it is mostly glass complexity and glass price, with calibration a minority. The modelling consequence is that insurer pressure on NAGS mark-ups, not only on calibration fees, is the variable to monitor.

Operating leverage

C-7562P-0160
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C+6010th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Material and two-sided. Belron carries high positive operating leverage (incremental margins of roughly 45 to 60 percent in H1 2026, because calibration drops through at close to 100 percent); Automotive carries high negative leverage (a decremental margin of about 24 percent on falling sales); TVH swings hard in both directions. At the group, a 50 basis-point change in the consolidated operating margin moves EPS by about 4.4 percent, so the range of outcomes for FY2027 to FY2028 EPS is perhaps plus or minus 8 to 10 percent around the central path from operating leverage alone.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron H1 2026 (derived): revenue about EUR 3,567m against EUR 3,414.5m (+EUR 153m reported); operating result EUR 820.3m against about EUR 730.5m (+EUR 90m): about 59 percent incremental reported, about 47 percent at constant currency. Sensitivity: plus or minus 1 point of Belron organic growth is plus or minus EUR 0.16 of EPS (1.2 percent).
  • Automotive H1 2026: sales minus 10.8 percent, adjusted operating result minus 58.7 percent (EUR 114.5m to EUR 47.3m): about 24 percent decremental (Bernstein models 25 percent); plus or minus 2 points of volume is plus or minus EUR 0.07 of EPS. Personnel-heavy retail cost base.
  • TVH: FY2025 margin fell 220 basis points on flat organic sales (ramp-up costs, negative leverage); H1 2026 sales +7.7 percent at constant currency produced PBT +17 percent.
  • Group: plus or minus 50 basis points of consolidated EBIT margin is plus or minus EUR 0.59 of EPS (4.4 percent), per the change-forces research.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C5711th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Operating leverage will keep lifting the group-share margin, but more slowly than in the last four years and from a dip in FY2026: the group-share adjusted operating margin goes from 11.3 percent (FY2025) to about 11.0 percent (FY2026), then 11.7, 12.3, 12.6 and 12.7 percent (FY2027 to FY2030), against a rise of about 55 basis points a year from FY2023 to FY2025. Two opposite leverage engines drive it. Belron's positive leverage is real but fading: its incremental margin falls from about 45 to 60 percent (H1 2026) to about 35 to 40 percent (FY2027 to FY2028) and about 30 percent (FY2029 to FY2030), because the highest-margin content (calibration and complex glass) grows more slowly as it saturates and insurers start to take part of it back. Automotive's negative leverage, which removed about EUR 140m of operating profit in FY2026, turns mildly positive from H2 2027 as restructuring removes fixed cost. The main lesson from the precedents is that Belron's leverage is symmetrical: the same cost structure that delivers 45 percent-plus on the way up cut operating profit by about 24 percent on a 4.4 percent organic sales decline in 2012, so the operating line is more sensitive to a volume relapse than its "GDP-agnostic" demand suggests.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron's cost structure (the evidence). Glass materials about 50 percent of job cost and labour 10 to 15 percent (Liberty Mutual former counsel, April 2026); direct labour 15 to 20 percent of sales (management, 9 September 2026); capital intensity about 1.5 percent of revenue (Fitch, December 2023); calibration carries 80 to 90 percent incremental gross margin once equipment and trained technicians exist (former Safelite regional vice president, January 2026). The cost base is variable in principle but sticky in practice: in 2012 a mild winter took organic sales down 4.4 percent and operating result down from EUR 262m to EUR 198m (minus 24 percent) because technician labour could not be cut fast enough; in H1 2025 margins were flat on 4 percent organic growth because direct labour was under-utilised and marketing was high; in 2020 the company flexed labour hard and kept EBITDA margin above 10 percent through a 17 percent volume fall. The implication: leverage is about 40 to 50 percent in both directions at the margin, and management's labour steering decides which end of that range is achieved.
  • Belron's recent leverage (derived). H1 2026: revenue about EUR 3,567m (+EUR 153m reported), operating result EUR 820.3m (+EUR 90m): 59 percent incremental reported, about 47 percent at constant currency. FY2025: about +EUR 263m revenue and about +EUR 177m operating result on 21.2 to 23.0 percent margins.
  • My Belron margin path and why the incremental margin falls. Margin 23.9 percent (FY2026), 24.7, 25.3, 25.6 and 25.8 percent; incremental margins 43, 38, 36, 31 and 30 percent. The fall comes from three sources, each evidenced: (i) calibration is only about 8 percent of revenue (Bernstein), so each five points of penetration adds under one point of revenue, and penetration growth slows as it approaches the company's "above 60 percent by FY2028"; (ii) US insurer caps and hourly-rate proposals (State Farm and GEICO cutting reimbursement, Travelers exploring a one-hour cap at about USD 200 against flat fees of USD 250 to 400) bite on rolling two-to-three-year contract renewals, so they reach the margin from 2027 onward; (iii) labour inflation and the cost of mobile calibration equipment absorb part of the gain. The company's FY2028 target of "above 25 percent" is met on my path with modest headroom; I do not take the H1 2026 run-rate (+160 basis points in a half) forward.
  • Automotive's leverage. H1 2026 sales minus 10.8 percent, operating result minus 58.7 percent (EUR 114.5m to EUR 47.3m): a decremental margin of about 24 percent, at the severe end of distributor precedents (Inchcape 2008 to 2009: like-for-like revenue minus 20 percent, EBIT minus 34 percent; retail margin 2.4 to 1.8 percent). D'Ieteren's own precedent is milder: in 2020 deliveries fell 19.2 percent and the margin only moved from 3.3 to 3.1 percent, and in H1 2020 gross margin rose (14.3 to 14.8 percent) on mix. The difference now is that price and mix are also negative (discounting against Chinese entrants, Volkswagen's EUR 6,000 ID discounts, end of subsidies), so there is no mix cushion. My path: margin 2.1 percent (FY2026), 2.6, 3.0, 3.1 and 3.1 percent, with revenue minus 10.4 percent (FY2026), minus 2.9 percent (FY2027), then +1 to 1.5 percent a year.
  • PHE and TVH. PHE's leverage is low and its margin moved only from 9.3 to 9.1 percent through 2025's 3.7 percent parts-price deflation, then to 9.6 percent in H1 2026; MEKO's margin fell from 4.9 to 2.7 percent and LKQ Europe's EBITDA margin from 10.0 to 4.6 percent (Q3 2026, ERP disruption), which shows how much better PHE's cost discipline is. I model PHE at 9.2 percent (FY2026) rising to 9.7 percent. TVH's leverage is high: margin minus 220 to 240 basis points on flat sales in FY2025, then PBT GS +17 percent on +7.7 percent constant-currency sales in H1 2026. I model 13.5 percent (FY2026) to 15.0 percent (FY2030), below its 16 percent FY2028 target.
  • Against the last four to six years. Belron's margin rose from 5.4 percent (2017) to 20.5 percent (2023), 21.2 percent (2024) and 23.0 percent (2025): about 150 to 180 basis points a year recently. My path adds about 80 basis points a year to FY2028 and about 25 basis points a year after: half the recent pace, then a quarter. The consolidated perimeter's company-adjusted EBIT margin (Automotive, PHE, Moleskine) was about 6.3 percent in FY2025 on my reconstruction; it falls to about 5.1 percent in FY2026 and recovers to 6.3 percent by FY2030, so the consolidated lines that FactSet shows will look flat over the five years while the group-share figures rise.

The range this creates. Belron alone: each 10 points of incremental margin on about EUR 400m of annual revenue growth is EUR 40m of operating result at 100 percent, EUR 20m at group share, about EUR 0.28 of EPS (1.6 percent of FY2028) per year, cumulating. Belron FY2028 margin plus or minus 100 basis points is plus or minus EUR 40m of PBT GS (about EUR 0.55 of EPS, 3.1 percent). Automotive margin plus or minus 50 basis points is plus or minus EUR 22m (EUR 0.30 of EPS). A 2012-style volume relapse at Belron (organic growth 3 points below plan for a year) would cost about EUR 100m of operating result at 100 percent and EUR 50m at group share (EUR 0.70 of EPS, 4 percent).

Disagreement with the first set. C-75 put the group EPS range from operating leverage at plus or minus 8 to 10 percent; I agree for FY2028 but would make it asymmetric (about minus 9 and plus 5 percent), because the incremental margins in consensus and in the company's target sit closer to the top of what Belron has shown than to the middle.

Financial leverage

C-7645P-0255
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F3626th of 30full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C-5512th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage now shapes EPS mainly as gearing, not as relief. The big relief step happened between H2 2025 and H1 2026 (Belron's derived finance costs down from about EUR 262m to EUR 208m a half at 100 percent) and is worth about EUR 50m of PBT GS in FY2026; after that, deleveraging of about EUR 550m to EUR 650m a year at Belron saves only about EUR 30m a year at 100 percent (EUR 15m at group share, about 1.3 percent of EPS a year). What remains large is the gearing effect: with about EUR 330m to EUR 345m of finance and other costs below a group-share operating result of EUR 1.3bn to 1.6bn, every 1 percent of operating growth becomes about 1.3 to 1.4 percent of PBT growth. On the balance sheet, Belron's leverage falls from 4.3x (June 2026) to about 3.9x (end-2026), 3.4x (end-2027) and 2.9x (end-2028); D'Ieteren's own consolidated net debt does not fall until FY2028, because the Spanish acquisitions, the PHE minority put, the buyback and the dividend absorb the cash. The first point at which leverage changes D'Ieteren's cash, rather than its accounting earnings, is when Belron's dividends step up after leverage goes below about 3x, which on my path is FY2029.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Belron's debt stack (company disclosures). Net debt EUR 8,342.9m at June 2026 (4.3x, post-IFRS 16; 4.5x December 2025; peak about 5.4x in 2024). Term Loans B due October 2031: USD 4,655m at SOFR plus 200 basis points and EUR 2,050m at Euribor plus 225 (after an automatic 25 basis-point step-down in October 2025 and the January 2026 repricing of minus 50 and minus 25 basis points). Senior secured notes due October 2029: EUR 850m at 4.625 percent and USD 1,115m at 5.75 percent. Ratings BB-/Ba3/BB, stable. Interest-rate and cross-currency swaps exist; the hedged share is not disclosed.
  • Belron finance-cost path (my estimate, 100 percent, including lease interest and other items below the operating result). EUR 516m (FY2025, derived; EUR 506.2m reported adjusted finance costs), EUR 416m (FY2026), EUR 385m, EUR 355m, EUR 335m and EUR 315m. The FY2025 to FY2028 saving of about EUR 160m at 100 percent matches Deutsche Bank's "over EUR 140m by FY2028". FY2027 and FY2028 savings assume net debt falls about EUR 600m a year at a marginal rate near 5.5 percent, partly offset by higher US short rates (a December Fed hike was above 90 percent priced on 23 October 2026). FY2029 and FY2030 include refinancing the 2029 notes (EUR 1.8bn equivalent) at about 1 to 1.5 points above today's coupons.
  • Why the relief shrinks. The FY2026 step combined the repricings, the step-down, a weaker dollar on USD debt and the first year of deleveraging. Later years have only deleveraging, at about EUR 30m a year at 100 percent. Belron also pays about EUR 200m a year of dividends and spent EUR 60m on 18 bolt-ons in 2025, which slows the pace.
  • Composition of EPS growth, FY2025 to FY2028 (derived from my base case). PBT GS rises EUR 310m: Belron operations +EUR 223m, Belron finance relief +EUR 81m, PHE +EUR 66m, TVH +EUR 17m, Automotive minus EUR 101m, Corporate, Immo and Moleskine +EUR 24m. In growth rates: group-share operating result +5.3 percent a year; PBT GS +9.8 percent a year, of which about 2.4 points is relief and about 2.1 points is gearing; EPS +10.9 percent a year with about 1.0 point from the share count.
  • EPS sensitivities. Plus 100 basis points on floating rates: about EUR 30m at 100 percent if about half of the EUR 6.0bn of term loans is unhedged (EUR 15m GS, EUR 0.21 of EPS, 1.2 percent of FY2028), up to EUR 60m (EUR 0.42, 2.4 percent) if none is hedged; the first set's figure (EUR 42m GS) assumed the whole EUR 8.3bn reprices, which overstates it because EUR 1.8bn is fixed to 2029 and about EUR 1bn is leases. One turn of leverage (about EUR 2.1bn of debt) at 6 percent is about EUR 125m at 100 percent, EUR 63m GS, EUR 0.87 of EPS. A ratings upgrade to BB+ and a further margin step-down would add perhaps EUR 15m to 25m at 100 percent (EUR 0.10 to 0.17 of EPS), an upside I do not include.
  • Other debt. PHE net financial debt EUR 1,394m (3.5x, June 2026, from EUR 1,120.7m and 3.0x in December 2025) after the EUR 200m add-on at Euribor plus 250; TVH EUR 901.4m (3.2x); Automotive EUR 259.5m (0.8x) after paying EUR 400m to Corporate in FY2025; Corporate EUR 299.8m (EUR 486.6m excluding inter-segment loans), loan-to-value under 10 percent. Put options to minorities: EUR 307.4m (June 2026, from EUR 119.1m, mostly the 49 percent of Polaris and Regueira) and EUR 194.0m over the 9 percent of PHE's holding company, maturing at the end of 2026.
  • D'Ieteren's consolidated balance sheet (FactSet basis, including leases, my estimate). Net debt EUR 1,942m (end-2025) to about EUR 2.14bn (end-2026), EUR 2.35bn (end-2027, assuming the PHE put is exercised for EUR 194m), then EUR 2.29bn, EUR 2.15bn and EUR 1.96bn. Consolidated net debt to consolidated EBITDA about 3.5x (FY2026) to 2.5x (FY2030); the ratio overstates the risk because it ignores the dividends from Belron and TVH, and understates it because Belron's EUR 8.3bn sits off it. Book equity rises by retained earnings including the equity-accounted results (consensus EUR 2,222m to EUR 3,481m by FY2028).
  • Listing and leverage. A listing as currently described (the private-equity holders sell; D'Ieteren keeps 50.3 percent) changes neither D'Ieteren's EPS nor its debt. A primary raise at Belron used to repay debt (Bernstein: Belron "could repurchase EUR 1.7bn in bonds or repay its Term Loans B post-IPO") would be roughly EPS-neutral for D'Ieteren if it did not subscribe (interest saved offsets the dilution of its stake) and would require about EUR 1bn of cash if it did; neither belongs in the base case.

Disagreement with the first set. C-76 called financial leverage "the single largest amplifier of both EPS growth and EPS risk" and the first set's group judgement said interest relief is "the largest single component of the consensus EPS growth path". The second half is no longer true from FY2027; the first half is true only in the sense of gearing. The forward risk from leverage is in the multiple (a junk-rated, floating-rate asset in a rising-rate regime) more than in EPS.

Cost-out scope NTM

C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C-4513th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Modestly. The only large discretionary cut under way is at Automotive (344 roles, sites, marketing and consulting), worth perhaps EUR 25 to 35m a year (my estimate; the company has not quantified it). Belron's cost base is largely variable (glass, technicians, vans) and its efficiency gains come from productivity and mix rather than cuts; TVH has temporary ramp-up and logistics costs that should fall away; PHE's lever is procurement synergy in Spain; the holding's 27-person centre has nothing to cut.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Automotive: 344 roles is 10 to 15 percent of the workforce; savings and timing subject to consultation (Deutsche Bank); personnel costs cited at EUR 305m (unverified figure).
  • Belron: about 38 percent of revenue is cost of goods (62.4 percent gross margin); the Transformation Program and technician productivity drove 160 basis points in one half; risks include technician churn and mobile-equipment cost.
  • TVH: Poland and US warehouse transition costs, Innovatis programme costs, freight; target margin 16 percent.
  • PHE: Spanish procurement integration; transport and personnel inflation absorbed so far.
  • Moleskine: cost-cutting under the co-CEOs; wholesale weakness in the US.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C+556th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. About EUR 45m to EUR 55m of identifiable cost savings reach PBT GS by FY2028, about 4 percent of FY2028 PBT GS, and almost none of it before H2 2027. The largest single item is the D'Ieteren Automotive plan (344 roles, site closures, cuts to marketing and consulting), worth about EUR 30m to 35m a year gross; Belgian consultation under the Renault Act and the habit of reinvesting part of the savings mean that roughly half reaches FY2027 and the run-rate arrives in FY2028, with about EUR 40m to 60m of cash restructuring costs in FY2027 that the adjusted figures exclude. The other items are smaller and spread out: TVH's dual-running and ramp-up costs falling away (about EUR 8m GS), PHE's Spanish procurement synergies (about EUR 8m to 10m GS by FY2029), Moleskine's cost reset (about EUR 5m), and the end of system-integrator fees, which helps reported earnings and cash but not adjusted earnings. At Belron, efficiency is embedded in the margin path and cannot be counted separately; it is not a cost-cutting story.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • D'Ieteren Automotive. The plan (announced September 2026) affects 344 roles, 10 to 15 percent of the workforce; the company has not quantified savings or timing, which remain "subject to consultation" (Deutsche Bank, 10 September 2026). My arithmetic: 344 roles at a fully loaded Belgian cost of about EUR 75,000 to 85,000 is EUR 26m to 29m a year, plus site and marketing savings of perhaps EUR 5m to 8m. Belgian collective-redundancy procedures typically run for several quarters before implementation (the Audi Brussels process began in July 2024); Deutsche Bank expects no material earnings benefit before mid-2027. Peer precedents show such programmes stick in part: Inchcape's GBP 90m of 2020 overhead savings, over half designed to be permanent; Bilia's SEK 70m of overhead cuts took the margin from minus 0.4 percent (2008) to 1.5 percent (2009); MEKO's SEK 100m programme and 500 job cuts (2025) were offset by double rents during warehouse moves. Offsets at D'Ieteren: Belgian wage indexation (about 2 to 3 percent a year on a personnel-heavy base), continuing share loss and lower volumes. Net, I put about EUR 15m of net benefit into FY2027 and about EUR 30m by FY2028, which is most of the move in my Automotive margin from 2.1 to 3.0 percent; the rest of the 4 percent FY2028 ambition would need volume and price recovery, which I do not assume.
  • TVH. H1 2026 adjusting items included EUR 9.1m of US warehouse closure and dual-running costs and EUR 4m of system-integrator fees (excluded from adjusted results), while ramp-up costs in Poland and Reno sat in the adjusted margin (minus 240 basis points in FY2025, Deutsche Bank). As the warehouses fill, about 100 to 150 basis points of margin returns over FY2027 to FY2028: about EUR 20m at 100 percent, EUR 8m GS. Peer recovery took 12 to 24 months (RS Group's Bad Hersfeld consolidation delivered over EUR 10m of annual savings after a period of customer attrition).
  • PHE. The Spanish deals (51 percent of Polaris and Regueira, about EUR 340m of 2025 sales) are expected to "halve the post-synergy transaction multiple" through procurement integration; on the put-option valuation (minority puts rose by about EUR 188m, implying about EUR 380m of equity value for 100 percent) the synergies needed are about EUR 20m to 25m of EBIT at 100 percent. At 51 percent within PHE's group share, that is about EUR 8m to 10m of PBT GS, phased over FY2027 to FY2029. The LKQ precedent (Q3 2026 European EBITDA cut by about USD 43m by a German ERP disruption) shows the integration risk is real.
  • Belron. The Transformation Program costs fell from EUR 124.1m (2023) to EUR 83.7m (2024); system-integrator fees were EUR 57.1m (2023), EUR 25.1m (2024) and EUR 44.3m (2025), all excluded from adjusted results. Their benefits are inside the margin path in P-01. When the fees stop (management implies with the 2028 target), reported earnings and cash improve by about EUR 40m a year at 100 percent (EUR 20m GS); adjusted earnings do not change.
  • Moleskine and Corporate. Moleskine's co-CEOs are cutting cost; I move Moleskine's operating margin from about 4 percent (FY2026) to about 8.5 percent (FY2030), against the company's 15 percent ambition. Corporate is already lean (27 people; Deutsche Bank cites a target corporate cost of minus EUR 10m to 15m by 2028); no saving there.
  • Timing summary (PBT GS, my estimate). FY2026: about zero net (savings not started; ramp-up costs still in). FY2027: about +EUR 25m (Automotive +15, TVH +5, PHE +3, Moleskine +2). FY2028: about +EUR 45m to 50m cumulative. FY2029 to FY2030: about +EUR 55m cumulative.

Disagreement with the first set. C-77 put the Automotive saving at EUR 25m to 35m a year; I agree on the gross figure but expect only about half to reach FY2027 and part to be absorbed by wage indexation, so the net benefit in the 18-month window is about EUR 15m.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4770P-0562
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?A742nd of 30full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B+602nd of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Three reinforcing loops at Belron dominate the next 18 months, and one balancing loop at Belron dominates the years after. The reinforcing loops (rising technical content pushing independents out and routing volume to Belron; the State Farm administration role steering claims to Safelite and deepening density and purchasing scale; deleveraging lowering borrowing cost and freeing cash for more deleveraging) are all measurable now and all act on FY2026 to FY2027 revenue, margin and finance costs. The balancing loop (Belron's margin on calibration and complex glass inviting insurer caps, documentation rules and hourly pricing) is formed but slow, because it travels through rolling contract renewals; it acts on the margin from FY2027 and is the main reason Belron's incremental margin falls in my model. At D'Ieteren Automotive a reinforcing downward loop (lower volume, fixed-cost deleverage, share loss, impairment) is being interrupted by a deliberate balancing action (the restructuring), which should floor the margin in FY2026 to FY2027 rather than reverse the volume decline. At the holding, the loop that matters for cash, rather than earnings, is weak today: Belron's dividends to D'Ieteren grow only once leverage falls below about 3x, so the cash that would fund larger buybacks arrives in FY2029 on my path. The net shape is a strong FY2026 to FY2027, a decelerating FY2028 to FY2030, and a cone that widens after FY2027.

The loops, one by one, with their effect on the line items.

  • Loop 1 (reinforcing, Belron share and revenue). More cameras per windscreen (penetration 45.9 to 52.1 percent in a year) raise the equipment and training bar (about USD 55,000 of ADAS equipment plus USD 18,775 a year of maintenance per site; 41 training hours a year per technician; Revv, December 2025); independents sublet or exit; insurers route more work to scaled networks (about 70 percent of jobs to large platforms, Bernstein); Belron's density and revenue per job rise. Evidence that it is running: Belron organic growth 7.3 percent against Boyd's minus 1 percent same-store sales (H1 2026); Boyd's calibration internalisation at 75 percent and calibration only 5 percent of its revenue. Precedent: vehicle complexity took the top ten US collision operators from 12 percent share (2014) to 21 percent (2020). What stalls it: penetration plateauing near the eligible-fleet limit, and self-calibration (not before about 2030 on the evidence). Model effect: about 1 point a year of share-driven volume on top of market volume; I use Belron volume of +3.0 percent (FY2027 and FY2028) and +2.5 to 2.75 percent after.
  • Loop 2 (reinforcing, Belron volume and cost per job). Safelite Solutions administers State Farm's National Glass Program from July 2025 (State Farm about 17 to 18 percent of US glass claims); competitors expect Safelite's share of State Farm jobs to move from 30 to 40 percent toward 70 to 80 percent; the routed volume deepens density, which lowers cost per job and supports purchasing terms (Belron is the largest or second-largest glass buyer, about 10 million units). What stalls it: anti-steering rules enforced by state regulators and insurers' wish for multiple providers; the files show no large carrier diverting volume since January 2026. Model effect: supports North American volume (+9.4 percent North American organic growth in H1 2026) through FY2027; I do not extrapolate it beyond, because the State Farm share gain is a one-time step.
  • Loop 3 (reinforcing, Belron finance costs). Cash generation lowers leverage, which triggers contractual margin step-downs (25 basis points in October 2025) and enables repricings (January 2026), which lower interest and raise free cash flow. Evidence: derived finance costs down about 18 percent year on year in H1 2026. What stalls it: higher reference rates, a dividend recapitalisation demanded by the private-equity holders (the September 2024 EUR 4.2bn precedent took leverage from about 3x to 5.4x), acquisitions, and the 2029 note refinancing. Model effect: finance costs EUR 516m to EUR 355m (FY2025 to FY2028, 100 percent), but with diminishing absolute savings (P-02). I treat a second dividend recapitalisation as unlikely before a listing, because it would undermine the listing; after a listing it becomes a real possibility, and the Consumer should treat it as the main threat to the deleveraging loop beyond FY2028.
  • Loop 4 (balancing, Belron price). Belron's margin rose from 5.4 to 23.0 percent largely by keeping scale economies; concentrated buyers see the margin and act (caps, ADAS Map, USAA documentation, Travelers hourly rates, Liberty Mutual within 14 months). Limits: no alternative national network, liability fear, the bundled administration-plus-repair offer. Model effect: price and mix fading to about 2 percent and incremental margin falling to about 30 percent by FY2030. This loop is the main reason I am about 1.5 percent below consensus for FY2028 and 10 percent below the two-broker FY2029 figure.
  • Loop 5 (balancing, Belron volume through premiums). Insurer cost pressure raises premiums and deductibles; drivers defer or pay cash at cheaper independents, where Safelite under-indexes. This loop ran in 2024 to H1 2025 (flat underlying volume, claims down about 15 percent) and reversed from H2 2025. With US consumer current conditions at the survey's lowest (October 2026) and insurers cutting calibration reimbursement to contain severity, it could re-engage in 2027. I treat it as the principal volume risk in the stress case, not in the base case.
  • Loop 6 (reinforcing downward, then balanced, Automotive). Falling registrations (400,000-unit plateau) and share (21.5 percent, minus 153 basis points) reduce scale and dealer viability; the fixed retail cost base deleverages (margin 4.5 to 2.1 percent); goodwill is impaired (EUR 47.2m); the restructuring is the balancing intervention. Precedent: dealer-density reductions restored surviving dealers' margins (China, 2025 to 2026, Morgan Stanley); Bilia's after-sales (73 to 75 percent of operating earnings) shows the defensive pool D'Ieteren under-exploits. What would break the floor: Volkswagen moving to agency terms, or a second leg down in fleet demand from 2027 tax changes. Model effect: revenue minus 2.9 percent in FY2027 and a margin floor near 2.1 to 2.6 percent, then about 3 percent.
  • Loop 7 (reinforcing, PHE scale). Acquisitions add purchasing volume, which improves supplier terms, which funds the next acquisition at a lower effective multiple ("halve the post-synergy multiple"). The counter-force is the low-cost Central European model (Inter Cars' selling, general and administrative costs at 14.4 percent of sales against LKQ's 28.7 percent) and integration risk. Model effect: PHE revenue +4 to 4.5 percent a year with bolt-ons, margin up about 10 basis points a year.
  • Loop 8 (cash to the holding: weak until FY2029). Belron's deleveraging should eventually turn into larger distributions; Belron already pays about EUR 200m a year (EUR 102.6m to D'Ieteren in H1 2026). I hold Belron's dividend to D'Ieteren near EUR 103m to 110m in FY2026 to FY2027, EUR 140m in FY2028, EUR 200m in FY2029 and EUR 260m in FY2030. The holding-discount loop (a listing narrowing the discount) works, on the precedents (Exor after the Ferrari placement, Prosus after Tencent sales, Grupo Sura), only when monetisation is paired with buybacks or distributions; D'Ieteren's buyback (about EUR 80m to 100m a year, about 1 percent of the market capitalisation) is too small to be that pairing.

Which loop is strongest. Over 18 months, Loop 3 for EPS (it explains about all of FY2026's growth) and Loop 1 for revenue. Over four years, Loop 4: it decides whether Belron's FY2028 to FY2030 margin is 25 or 26.5 percent, which is a swing of about EUR 60m of PBT GS (EUR 0.85 of EPS) by FY2030. The insurer pushback is strong enough to slow the share-and-margin loop at Belron but, on the evidence, not to stall it inside the forecast horizon.

Deutsche Bank: group-share PBT EUR 984.2m. Jefferies EUR 14.48, ING EUR 14.20, KBC EUR 13.90.

Case simplicity
