P-09After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?D5215th of 30full answer under 7b Margins/growth vs. past
I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

B+545th of 30

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Consensus for the next 18 months is roughly where it should be and has stopped falling; the path from here is a shallow dip in March 2027 followed by a recovery, with the net breadth of revisions turning from negative to positive in the second half of 2027. FY2026 (median EPS EUR 13.40 against our EUR 13.63) drifts up 1 to 2 percent into and after the March results. FY2027 (median EUR 15.56, mean EUR 15.35, against our EUR 15.60) is cut 2 to 4 percent after the cautious FY2027 framing on 10 March 2027, then recovers to about where it is now by the end of 2027 as the first half delivers about plus 13 percent and guidance is confirmed or raised (probability of a net upgrade to FY2027 over the full 18 months about 35 percent, of a hold within plus or minus 3 percent about 40 percent, of a net cut about 25 percent). FY2028 (median EUR 17.93 against our EUR 17.68) drifts down 1 to 3 percent as the Automotive, TVH and Moleskine ambitions are softened, partly offset by Belron. Beyond 18 months the picture is less favourable: the two-broker FY2029 figure of EUR 21.55 is about 10 percent above our EUR 19.32 and will be cut as more brokers publish, because it extrapolates FY2027 to FY2028 growth rates that the fading of both Belron engines does not support. The revision breadth today (the dashboard's score of minus 5; FactSet mean EPS down 12.3 percent for FY2026 and 10.9 percent for FY2027 over twelve months, but down only 0.6 and 0.2 percent over one month) is the picture of a cutting cycle that has ended, not of one that continues; the 79 percent buy share (down 6 points in a month) is the other side of the Wisdom Library's "Buy rating against revisions divergence": the sell-side values the parts and has been cutting the year.

Why the cuts have ended, and what starts the upgrades.

  • The cuts were Automotive and TVH; they are in. Bernstein trimmed group PBT 2 percent and Deutsche Bank 1.2 percent after the H1 2026 results; Jefferies cut TVH's operating forecasts by more than 20 percent in September 2026; Bernstein models Automotive at 1.7 percent by FY2028, Deutsche Bank near 4 percent. The remaining disagreement is on a segment worth about 7 percent of FY2026 PBT GS. Belron estimates cluster within about 1 percent of consensus and have been drifting up (Bernstein raised stand-alone Belron estimates after H1 2026).
  • The one-month revisions are flat. FY2026 minus 0.6 percent, FY2027 minus 0.2 percent, FY2028 plus 0.1 percent; target prices minus 2 percent in a month, flat over three; the median target of EUR 230 is 34 percent above the price. The dashboard's earnings-momentum F reflects the twelve-month history, not the current slope.
  • What moves FY2026 up: the March 2027 print. Our EUR 13.63 against a EUR 13.40 median; Deutsche Bank's EUR 13.40 is the anchor, Jefferies' EUR 14.48 the top. A result of about EUR 990m to 1,000m of PBT GS lifts the median toward EUR 13.6.
  • What moves FY2027 down in March: the framing. The precedent is March 2026, when consensus fell about 5 percent over the following quarter after a guidance 7 to 9 percent below it. A "high-single-digit" framing against a consensus of plus 14 percent in PBT GS implies cuts of 2 to 4 percent, smaller than in 2026 because brokers already model the divergence and because the Belron number printed the same day will be strong.
  • What moves FY2027 up from mid-2027: delivery. H1 2027 PBT GS of about EUR 547m (plus 13 percent) against an H1 2026 base of EUR 482.4m; an easy Automotive comparison from H2; the Spanish full year at PHE; a possible raise in September 2027. The second set's base case has FY2027 at consensus; delivery at that level after a cautious framing produces upgrades back to it, and the market will read them as upgrades.
  • What moves FY2028 down slowly: the ambitions. Consensus FY2028 EPS of EUR 17.93 embeds some of the company's non-Belron targets; as they are softened, estimates drift down 1 to 3 percent; Belron at 25.3 to 26 percent offsets part of it. Our EUR 17.68 is 1.4 percent below the median, which is where the drift ends.

The breadth of revisions and the ratings.

  • Revision breadth turns neutral by the FY2026 results (the Automotive cuts are done; the FY2027 cuts in March are concentrated in a few weeks) and positive from September 2027 (probability about 55 percent that the net ratio is positive for the FY2027 and FY2028 years by then). The dashboard's score should move from minus 5 toward zero by spring 2027 and positive in the autumn.
  • Ratings breadth is more likely to fall than rise: 79 percent buy with all ten visible brokers at Buy or Overweight leaves no room for upgrades; a downgrade on valuation after a listing-driven re-rating, or on the March framing, is the likelier move. This is not an earnings signal.
  • Dispersion narrows as the two brokers on other bases (EUR 9.81 and 10.00 for FY2026) either re-base or drop out; the median is the right anchor throughout, not the mean.

The 18-month-to-three-year horizon. FY2029 consensus (EUR 21.55, two brokers) implies PBT GS of about EUR 1,540m and growth of about 20 percent from FY2028; our FY2029 is EUR 1,371m (plus 8.3 percent), because Belron's price and mix fades toward 2 percent, finance relief is largely spent, and the 2029 notes refinance at a higher coupon. As coverage of FY2029 broadens from two brokers to eight or ten, the median will fall toward EUR 19.5 to 20.5 (about minus 5 to 10 percent). Belron's margin could run above our 25.6 percent (Bernstein 26 percent) and partly offset this; a post-listing recapitalisation would make it worse. The three-year consensus therefore has a downward skew that the 18-month consensus does not, and the memo's topic 6 should say so.

What would change this view. A coordinated US insurer move on calibration or NAGS pricing in 2027 (cuts to FY2028 of 3 to 5 percent, led by Bernstein); a US consumer relapse with a mild winter (FY2027 cuts of 3 to 5 percent in March to May 2027); a Belron recapitalisation after a listing (FY2028 to FY2029 cuts of 5 to 10 percent); on the upside, Belron at 25 percent in FY2027 with volume at 3 percent plus (FY2027 to FY2028 upgrades of 2 to 4 percent, breadth strongly positive).

Why the score is at the middle. The gap between our assessment and consensus is small for FY2026 to FY2028 (plus 1.7, plus 0.3, minus 1.4 percent) and negative for FY2029; the favourable element is timing: the revision cycle is at its trough, and the next twelve months contain the two events (the FY2026 beat and the H1 2027 delivery) that turn breadth positive, with one scheduled cut between them. For the memo's topic 8 (momentum), the honest statement is that sell-side earnings momentum is weak now, bottoming, and likely to improve from the second quarter of 2027.

D-0758
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

B+603rd of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Mostly idiosyncratically now, and the multiple is company-driven. Over the latest six-month window (11 May to 2 November 2026) the sector basket explains 19 percent of D'Ieteren's daily variance (correlation 0.44, beta 0.60) and 81 percent is the stock's own; the stock returned plus 1.1 percent against the basket's plus 6.8 percent. The co-movement is cyclical rather than stable: the rolling R-squared has swung between 2 to 6 percent (the turns of 2024 to 2025 and 2025 to 2026) and 44 to 51 percent (June to August 2025 and July to August 2026), rising in the months around the sector's results seasons and the market's rotations and falling when company news dominates, as it has since the 9 September 2026 triple announcement (the Belron review, the chief executive change, the Automotive miss). No single peer explains more than 14 percent of the variance (Sixt), and the two halves of the sector that the first set contrasted explain little (Inchcape 9 percent, Inter Cars 1 percent), so the first set's judgement that the shares "trade with the car-retail half" should be replaced: the shares trade on D'Ieteren's own news and on the Belron event, and their underperformance of the sector over six months is idiosyncratic. The level of the multiple, however, sits where the sector's does: a trailing P/E of 9.9x at the 21st percentile of the stock's own ten-year range, against Inchcape at the 9th, Sixt at the 5th, Auto1 at the 1st, Ayvens at the 16th, Bilia at the 34th, ZIGUP at the 50th and Inter Cars at the 60th. So the day-to-day movement is company-driven, the de-rating since 2024 is shared with the sector, and the premium or discount within that de-rated band is set by company factors: the holding discount (37 to 45 percent), the 2024 recapitalisation, the event, the governance.

What the pattern means for predictability and for the memo.

  • The multiple will respond to company delivery and to the event, not to sector rotation. A beta of 0.60 and an R-squared of 19 percent mean a sector recovery (the regime note's trigger is a US 10-year below 4.5 percent) would lift D'Ieteren about 0.6 for 1 on the days it happens and would not by itself close the gap; equally, a further sector sell-off would hurt it less than peers. The case is therefore self-determined to an unusual degree: the 10 March 2027 results, the Belron process and the Automotive consultation will set the multiple. This is positive for predictability (the drivers are the ones analysed here) and it places the burden of the return on company events rather than on a sector tailwind.
  • The co-movement peaks are a timing signal. The two windows in which the stock moved with the sector (mid-2025, mid-2026) were the half-year results seasons for the sector and the periods of sector-wide de-rating; in both the stock's beta rose toward 0.9 to 1.0. The next such window is February to March 2027 (sector full-year results, D'Ieteren's 10 March). If the sector is weak into that window, D'Ieteren's own result will be read through the sector's lens for a few weeks; the memo should not mistake that for a change in the company's standing.
  • The multiple's own history is the better anchor than peers'. The 24-month-forward P/E of 10.0x is at the 23rd percentile of five years (median 11.17x, range 8.19x to 18.65x) and the ten-year bands put "prudent" at 11.0x and "crash" at 9.3x. Peer multiples (Inchcape 7.1x, Ayvens 5.8x) are not fair comparators for a business that is two-thirds insurer-paid glass repair; Boyd Group and Driven Brands (about 17x forward EV/EBIT) and Cary Group (26x EBITA at its 2022 buyout; an exit mooted above EUR 3bn in 2026) are the comparators for the asset that carries the value, and they are not in the dashboard sector.
  • The idiosyncratic share is also the risk. 81 percent of variance from company news means a disappointing event (a delayed or under-priced listing, a cautious FY2027 framing, a kitchen-sink) moves the stock without any cushion from the sector; the first set's C-82 perception risks stand, and the co-movement data say they would be felt in full.

Why the score is above the middle. A company-driven multiple on a case whose company drivers are well understood, monitorable and currently delivering (Belron) is a favourable condition for predictability; it also means that the valuation reset the first set describes (23rd percentile) was done to the stock for its own reasons (the 2024 transaction, Automotive, the event's uncertainty), which company actions can reverse. The negative is that the stock will not be carried by its sector if the company stumbles.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the eleven ratings page uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D4821st of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Five patterns apply, and D'Ieteren fits the outperformance patterns operationally and the underperformance patterns structurally, which is why its shares have lagged its fundamentals. The decisive precedent for the second set is that a listing of a holding's main asset rarely closes a holding discount on its own; what closes it is a channel from the asset's value to the holding's shareholders (cash distributions or buybacks funded by the asset).

The patterns.

  • 1. In this sector, non-manufacturer-dependent aftermarket and services outperform; single-manufacturer distribution underperforms. In the twelve months to October 2026: Inter Cars +92.4 percent, ZIGUP +32.5 percent, Sixt +0.7 percent against Inchcape minus 16.5 percent, Ayvens minus 8.6 percent, Auto1 minus 39.6 percent. The Researcher's sector note (6 October 2026) treats single-carmaker distributors and franchised dealers as structural shrinkers. D'Ieteren is about four-fifths in the first group by profit, but its shares (+10.3 percent over twelve months, minus 4.8 percent over three, relative strength 31st percentile) trade with the second group. Rule of thumb: a mixed group trades with its weaker half in risk-off and with its stronger half only after a catalyst separates them.
  • 2. Penetration-driven content growth sustains above-market growth and margin expansion until saturation, after which the multiple compresses before the earnings slow. Belron's margin from 5.4 percent (2017) to 23.0 percent (2025), about 6 points from calibration mix. Fit: strong now; the risk window is when penetration approaches its plateau (above 60 percent by FY2028) and investors start to price the terminal rate.
  • 3. Distributors' operating leverage is severe in downturns. Inchcape: a 20 percent like-for-like revenue fall in 2008 to 2009 cut EBIT 34 percent; European distributor peers guide profit declines of 10 to 15 percent in this cycle against D'Ieteren Automotive's 40 percent-plus (the risks research flags this as a bottom-5-percent outlier). Fit: Automotive is the extreme case because it combines a cyclical normalisation with a structural plateau and a share loss.
  • 4. European holding discounts persist through cycles (GBL and Sofina at 28 to 32 percent; D'Ieteren 37 to 45 percent) and narrow only on catalysts that give both a price and a clock (Wisdom Library: "A Catalyst Needs a Price and a Clock"). A listing gives a price; the clock is the private-equity sellers'. General precedent outside the files: Naspers and Prosus carried large discounts to their listed Tencent stake for years and narrowed them only when they began selling Tencent shares to fund buybacks. Fit: D'Ieteren's discount has structural components (family control, no synergies, leverage) that a listing does not remove; the Consumer should not assume a listing alone closes the gap.
  • 5. Debt-funded recapitalisations followed by deleveraging produce EPS growth the market does not capitalise (Wisdom Library: "leverage-to-eps-transmission"). About 40 percent of the prior memo's FY2025 to FY2028 EPS growth was finance-cost relief. Fit: exact; expect the market to pay a lower multiple for that part of the growth.
  • Company's own precedent. 22.0 percent annualised total shareholder return over ten years, built on Belron's margin and the TVH and PHE acquisitions funded by Belron capital reductions; the share price "lacked a clear catalyst" until September 2026.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

B+684th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. About 12 to 18 percent in a base trough (EUR 140 to 150) and about 25 percent in a stress case that combines a trough multiple with an earnings cut (about EUR 130). The downside is bounded by the stock's own multiple history, which is already near its low, and by a sum of the parts at conservative multiples, not by Belron alone. Against it, a return to the ten-year median multiple without any event is worth about +10 percent and to the 75th percentile about +21 percent, so the price-only asymmetry is roughly balanced, and the listing is what tilts it.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Today: EUR 171.3; 24-month-forward EPS about EUR 17.13; 24-month-forward P/E 10.04x (23rd percentile of five years; median 11.17x; five-year range 8.19x to 18.65x); dashboard P/E 9.9x at the 21st percentile of ten years.
  • Bands (ten-year): 10th percentile 9.3x gives EUR 159 (minus 7 percent); five-year low 8.19x gives EUR 140 (minus 18 percent); 52-week low EUR 141.8; the risks research's downside fair value EUR 145 (35 percent discount, Belron at 13.3x EV/EBIT).
  • Stress: 24-month-forward EPS cut about 8 percent (the refinancing, currency and tax case) to about EUR 15.8 at 8.2x gives about EUR 129 (minus 25 percent). A peer-style multiple (Inchcape at 7.1x) is not a fair trough for this mix but shows the tail.
  • Cover arithmetic (prior memo): Belron's stake covers the market capitalisation only at about 16.6x EBIT or more; at the 2017 transaction multiple (13.3x) it covers about 72 percent and the other assets supply the rest.
  • Upside without the event: 11.0x gives about EUR 188; 12.1x about EUR 207.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

B555th of 30

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative is a single-event story with an earnings compounder underneath. The market currently prices the car-retail half and the uncertainty of the event, not the compounding of Belron; the staircase that could change this has one large, novel, externally driven step (the Belron liquidity event) surrounded by smaller steps that are either transitional (a new CEO, a restructuring) or already known (the FY2028 targets). The step that would do the most for the holding discount is not the listing itself but a channel from Belron's value to D'Ieteren's shareholders (Belron distributions or larger buybacks), which nobody has yet promised. For the second set, the narrative implies modelling the earnings path independently of the event and treating the event as a valuation input with explicit odds and timing.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–66

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Congruent in architecture and direction, with five identifiable contradictions, all of which can be resolved, and all of which resolve in the same direction: the earlier work slightly understated Belron and the holding's below-the-line costs, and overstated the role of finance costs and the company's reticence about raising guidance. The first set's external and internal pictures ("good business weather, bad market weather"; a holding moving from building and levering to deleveraging and crystallising), its industry and competitive-advantage judgements (a dominant leader over a fragmented tail, fragile only to the insurer and to self-calibration) and its risk treatment (one correlated stress case) feed straight into the second set's model, which is operating-led, Belron-concentrated and conservative against the company's non-Belron ambitions. The fourth set's research reports confirm the shape of both. The contradictions are in the arithmetic of segments, in the share of growth attributed to interest relief, in the guidance-behaviour record and in a few data points; none changes the base case total, and together they make the case more legible, not less, because once corrected the three sets say the same thing: the forecasts depend on Belron's operating result and an Automotive floor, and the market's narrative depends on an event that does not change either.

The contradictions, and how I resolve them.

  • Segment arithmetic (first set wrong, second set inherited it). The H1 2026 PBT GS figures for PHE (97.3) and TVH (33.1) were 91 percent and 75 percent of the company's 106.8 and 44.2; the residual of plus 7.4 was therefore wrong (true: Corporate and Immo minus 8.5, Moleskine minus 4.7). H2 2025 Automotive PBT GS was about EUR 106m, not EUR 80m. Resolution: the second set's FY2026 total of EUR 992m stands because its Belron (638) and TVH (75) lines were conservative by about the amount its residual (minus 1) was optimistic; my FY2026 composition is Belron about 648 to 650, PHE 206, TVH about 80, Automotive 73, Corporate and Immo about minus 14, Moleskine about minus 3, total about EUR 990m to 993m. For FY2027 and FY2028 the same re-slicing nets to within EUR 5m of the Analyst's totals. The lesson for the Consumer: build the memo's segment tables from the company's own PBT GS lines (the six figures above), not from the first set's.
  • Finance-cost relief (first set 40 percent of FY2025 to FY2028 EPS growth; one AlphaSense report "about half"; second set 26 percent, and 11 percent of FY2026 to FY2028). The second set is right and the fourth set's reports show why the first set and the sell-side framed it as they did: Jefferies and Deutsche Bank both describe interest reduction as "a primary driver" because they are looking at FY2026 against FY2025, where it is. From FY2027 the growth is operational. This is the single most useful correction for the memo's topic 5 (operating leverage) and topic 7 (scope for upgrades): the market will discover in 2027 that D'Ieteren's growth is not financial engineering.
  • Guidance behaviour (first set: "does not upgrade during the year"; fourth-set research: raised in September 2022 and September 2023). The first set's archetype ("sandbagging precision", dependable for avoiding misses, uninformative about the upside) is half right: the company does set low bars, but it has lifted them mid-year twice in four years, both times when Belron was running far ahead and the rest of the portfolio was clean. 2026 is a year in which it did not, because Automotive was collapsing. The resolution matters for I-01: a 2027 in which Belron keeps compounding and Automotive has an easy second-half comparison looks more like 2022 to 2023 than like 2026.
  • Belron's distributions (first set: "could resume distributions" below 3x; second set: already pays). The fourth set confirms the second set and adds the history (EUR 1.8bn in 2021, about EUR 0.8bn in 2022, about EUR 1.5bn in 2023, EUR 4.2bn in 2024, about EUR 0.4bn a year run-rate now). The constraint the earlier sets did not have is Fitch's: an upgrade needs a post-dividend free cash flow margin above 2 percent, and Fitch expects continued "generous" dividends to hold it near 1 percent. So the dividend path and the ratings-upgrade path pull against each other; the second set's model (dividends to D'Ieteren flat at EUR 103m to 110m to FY2027, rising only from FY2028 as leverage approaches 3x) is the consistent middle path, and I keep it.
  • Price and mix composition (first set: "mostly mix, calibration"; second set: mostly complex glass priced off NAGS, calibration only 8 percent of revenue). The fourth set's reports side with the second set (Bernstein: 8 percent of FY2025 sales; traditional replacement about EUR 400, calibration about EUR 150; the September 2025 NAGS update lifted list prices 5.6 percent, a tailwind that annualises in H2 2026). The monitoring consequence stands: watch the NAGS mark-up and insurer flat-rate caps (USD 390 to 550) as much as the calibration fee.
  • Smaller points. PHE's term-loan add-on is at Euribor plus 250 (second set), not 300. The rate sensitivity of EUR 42m at group share per 100 basis points (first set) assumed the whole stack reprices; with 77 percent floating and an undisclosed hedge the range is EUR 15m to 30m at group share (second set), which I keep. Belron's USD Term Loan B is now USD 4,036m, EUR 0.5bn-equivalent below the second set's figure, which supports the deleveraging path. The first set's "shares trade with the car-retail half" is contradicted by the co-movement table (D-07).

What jars but is not a contradiction. The first set's management judgement ("take Belron's targets at face value, lean slightly above FY2026 guidance") and the second set's lean (Belron up, Automotive, TVH and Moleskine down) are consistent, but the second set's Belron FY2026 margin of 23.9 percent is below what its own stance implies now that H2 2025 is known to have been 24.4 percent: a half-year that is seasonally the stronger, with a NAGS tailwind still running, should not fall 160 basis points short of a run-rate that H1 2026 lifted by 160 basis points. I place FY2026 at about 24.1 to 24.2 percent (about EUR 10m of PBT GS above the Analyst) and FY2027 at about 24.8 percent, and absorb the difference in the segment re-slicing rather than in an input change, because the total is unchanged.

Why congruence matters for predictability. Three independent lines of work (the foundations, the model, the fourth-set research built from brokers, filings and expert calls) converge on the same two operating drivers and the same two risks, and the corrections made here are all to levels, not to directions. A case whose parts disagree about direction is unpredictable whatever the quality of the forecast; this one does not.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

B+605th of 30

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The narrative is comprehensive about the event and the asset, and thin about the earnings. The three houses whose research the fourth-set report could read (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Bernstein; of the ten brokers FactSet shows, the report credited views only to these three, so the coverage check is complete for what AlphaSense holds and partial for the market) articulate fully and in numbers: Belron's deleveraging path and finance-cost reduction; the Belron listing as the catalyst and the fact that a secondary sale brings D'Ieteren no cash; calibration penetration and its margin effect; the holding discount (37 to 45 percent) and its peer comparisons; and, at Bernstein, the 8 percent revenue share of calibration, the fee-against-glass distinction and the basis differences that inflate FactSet's dispersion. Investor commentary that the report could find is confined to holders' letters (AVI Global Trust: a discount near 50 percent and the listing as the unlock) and a Berenberg note warning that a listing could widen the discount; no Twitter or forum commentary was found, which for a Belgian mid-cap with EUR 20m to 25m of daily turnover is itself a finding: the stock has no retail narrative to speak of, and its narrative is the sell-side's. What the sell-side under-articulates or omits, and which our work treats as central: the composition of growth (every house frames interest relief as "a primary driver", which is true for FY2026 and false for FY2027 to FY2028, when operations carry about 90 percent); the insurer re-basing of calibration and NAGS pricing (present in expert calls, absent from broker models; Bernstein notes the "investor debate" but models price and mix of 3 to 4 percent a year through 2029); the size and timing of Automotive's savings (nobody has a number); the EUR 501m of put options (deducted in sum-of-the-parts tables, never discussed as a 2026 to 2027 cash call); Belron's recurring adjusting items as a quality-of-earnings question (Bernstein occasionally); the anticipated-acquisition method (nobody); the hedge ratio (acknowledged as unknown); Fitch's dividend constraint on the upgrade path (nobody); the precedent that holding discounts narrow only when monetisation is paired with buybacks (Jefferies via Exor and Prosus; Berenberg's warning; not the consensus view, which treats the listing itself as the unlock). The result is a narrative that already contains our positives and prices them in its Buy ratings and EUR 230 median target, that does not contain our two negatives for 2027 to 2028 (pricing, Automotive execution), and that attributes the discount's closing to an event that our work says is necessary but not sufficient.

Dynamic by dynamic: who says it, how prominently, with what view.

  • Belron finance costs for 2027 to 2028 (widely discussed; direction right, emphasis wrong). Jefferies (21 January 2026, 10 September 2026): leverage to 3.8x by end-2026, the January repricing worth a 2 percent upgrade to PBT, interest reduction "a primary driver of near-term earnings expansion". Deutsche Bank (15 July 2026): leverage toward investment grade by 2028, a three-year PBT CAGR of 16 percent "as leverage costs dissipate". Bernstein (25 September 2026): 77 percent floating, hedges roll off, cuts matter. None states the operating-against-financial split for FY2027 to FY2028; our 89 to 11 split is not in the narrative. Well known as a direction; misattributed as a share.
  • US insurer calibration and documentation changes (known to experts, thin in research). Expert calls (former Gerber, Glass America, Protech, Travelers, Liberty Mutual, GEICO, Nationwide; February to September 2026) describe flat rates of USD 390 to 550, the ADAS Map requirement, hourly-rate studies and reimbursement denials without documentation. Bernstein (19 May 2026) names insurer pushback as an investor debate; Deutsche Bank (15 July 2026) argues scale protects Belron; Jefferies (21 August 2026) flags self-calibration as a long-term risk. No house models a price effect. Known qualitatively to specialists, not priced in models: this is the dynamic with the largest gap between evidence and narrative.
  • Calibration's share of revenue and the fee-against-glass distinction (one house). Bernstein only (15 April and 19 May 2026): 8 percent of revenue; EUR 150 against EUR 400; margin above 50 percent. The consequence (that the exposure runs through NAGS mark-ups on 84 percent of revenue as much as through calibration fees) is drawn by nobody.
  • Belron already pays dividends; a secondary listing brings no cash (well understood). Deutsche Bank (dividends above EUR 300m in FY2025 while deleveraging), Bernstein (27 August 2026: a secondary sale by the private-equity holders, no capital to the parent), Jefferies (D'Ieteren keeps 50.3 percent). Known.
  • Automotive transformation savings (not quantified by anyone). Deutsche Bank (10 September 2026): 344 roles, 10 to 15 percent of the workforce, "timing and size highly uncertain, subject to consultation". Bernstein models a 1.7 percent FY2028 margin without stating savings. The gap is universal and the company's.
  • Put options (not discussed as cash). Deutsche Bank deducts EUR 317m in its sum of the parts; Jefferies adjusts net asset value; nobody schedules the end-2026 exercise or notes that the strike rises with PHE's EBITDA. Unknown as a dynamic.
  • Adjusting items and quality of earnings (barely). Bernstein occasionally; the company's defence (audited, non-recurring) is taken at face value. Reported EPS at 35 to 40 percent below adjusted is visible to anyone who looks; few do.
  • The new chief executive on buybacks against acquisitions (broadly framed, no view). Jefferies and the company's own statements: "organic investment, bolt-ons and distributions"; the EUR 100m buyback reinstated at end-2025. Investors are waiting for the AGM in May 2027 or a strategy statement. No position taken.
  • The holding discount and the listing (widely discussed; split on mechanism). Jefferies: 45 percent to a EUR 317 net asset value; Deutsche Bank: 45 percent, "excessively wide" against 25 percent for Exor, GBL, Sofina; Bernstein: a 15 to 25 percent discount to the Belron stake alone, the listing "a highly credible standalone catalyst"; Jefferies cites Exor's 10 percent buyback and Prosus's Tencent-funded programme (discount to 28 percent) as the precedents; Berenberg (12 February 2026) warns that direct access to a listed Belron reduces the need to own the parent and could widen the discount unless met with buybacks. The consensus leans to "the listing unlocks"; our view (a price and a clock, but the discount's governance component remains until Belron's cash reaches D'Ieteren's shareholders) is the minority view, held by Berenberg and implied by Jefferies' precedents.
  • Peers and sector framing. Boyd Group and Driven Brands are discussed for claims volumes (flat to down 2 percent) and internalisation of calibration; Cary Group's exit (above EUR 3bn mooted; 26x EBITA at its 2022 buyout) is noted as a valuation benchmark; Inchcape, Bilia and Auto1 frame car retail as under pressure and service as the buffer; Inter Cars and LKQ frame parts distribution as resilient but execution-prone; Exor, GBL, Sofina and Prosus frame the discount. The sector narrative (October 2026) favours parts and services over car retail and leasing; D'Ieteren is discussed as a Belron proxy, not as a car retailer, which is the correct frame and the one the share price does not yet reflect.

What is well known, what is partly known, what is unknown (for the memo's topic 7 and topic 8).

  • Well known and in the price of the sell-side's targets, if not of the shares: Belron's compounding and margin target, its deleveraging, the listing as the catalyst, the holding discount, the Automotive collapse and the segment divergence.
  • Partly known: insurer pricing pressure (as a debate, not a number), Belron's H2 2025 margin of 24.4 percent (Jefferies), the frequency gap (1.5 against 2.1 percent, Bernstein), the premium deflation (Bernstein), self-calibration (Jefferies, as a long-dated risk), the buyback-pairing precedent (Jefferies, Berenberg).
  • Unknown or unarticulated: the operating composition of FY2027 to FY2028 growth; the NAGS mark-up exposure; the Automotive savings number and the Renault Act timing; the put cash and its strike; the hedge ratio and the 2029 refinancing cost; Fitch's dividend constraint; the anticipated-acquisition method; the fact that the company raised guidance mid-year in 2022 and 2023.

The secondary horizon (18 months to three years). The sell-side's narrative for 2028 to 2029 is Belron at 25 to 26 percent, leverage below 3x, a listed Belron, a narrowed discount and EPS growth in the mid-teens (the two-broker FY2029 figure). Our view differs in two places that the narrative does not carry: growth slows to 7 to 9 percent as price and mix and finance relief fade, and the 2029 refinancing resets Belron's fixed coupons upward. The narrative is therefore more optimistic than we are beyond 18 months, and the gap is unfavourable for a case that depends on the three-year consensus holding.

Why the score is below the middle. The purpose of this question for the memo is to find expected developments that guidance, estimates and the narrative do not capture. On the positive side, the narrative captures most of what we expect (Belron, deleveraging, the event) and has already converted it into unanimous Buy ratings and a 34 percent upside to target; the one positive it misses (that FY2027 growth is operational) is a refinement, not a new thesis. On the negative side, it misses the two dynamics that could make 2027 to 2028 fall short (pricing, Automotive execution) and over-reads the listing's effect on the discount. A narrative that already contains the bull case and omits the bear case leaves the gap between our assessment and the narrative tilted against the case in terms of surprise, even though the share price (10x earnings, a 45 percent discount, technical momentum D) has not followed the narrative, which is a separate and more favourable fact that belongs to the memo's topic 11.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 30 October 2026, 04:51 to 05:11 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
D'Ieteren Group (DIE-BE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Prepared 2026-10-29 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years). Price EUR 171.3 (2026-10-29).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading guide for the Consumer: the five things that decide how to model this company

  1. Model the group in group share, not on the FactSet consolidated lines. D'Ieteren holds 50.3 percent of Belron (vehicle glass repair, replacement and recalibration) and 40 percent of TVH (industrial spare parts); both are equity-accounted. The FactSet block's revenue (EUR 8,034m), EBITDA (EUR 622m) and EBIT (EUR 392m, 4.9 percent) for FY2025 cover only D'Ieteren Automotive, PHE and Moleskine. The company's own measure, used for guidance and by the sell-side, is the proportionate "group share": revenue EUR 12,082.1m and adjusted operating result EUR 1,367.1m (11.3 percent) in FY2025, adjusted profit before tax, group share (adjusted PBT GS) EUR 955.6m. Belron was about 57 percent of the FY2025 group-share adjusted operating result (about EUR 778m of 1,367m) and about 77 to 78 percent of sum-of-the-parts value.
  2. The working EPS identity. Brokers' adjusted EPS of EUR 12.95 in FY2025 reconciles to adjusted PBT GS x (1 - about 28 percent tax) / about 53m shares (955.6 x 0.72 / 53.1 = 12.96). Use this identity for the forecast: build segment adjusted PBT at group share, subtract corporate costs and interest, tax at about 28 percent (the company does not guide the rate; it is implied), divide by a share count falling about 1 percent a year. Do not use the brokers'-basis EPS history for FY2023 (EUR 10.05) and FY2024 (EUR 11.99): it does not reconcile with adjusted PBT GS of EUR 970.8m and EUR 1,065.0m, and FactSet flags the earlier years as restated.
  3. Segment architecture. Six units: Belron (50.3 percent; 55 percent of its revenue North American, largely unhedged in translation), D'Ieteren Automotive (100 percent; exclusive Volkswagen Group importer in Belgium), PHE (91 percent; Western European independent car-parts distribution), TVH (40 percent; forklift and industrial parts), Moleskine (100 percent; about 1 percent of profit, loss-making), D'Ieteren Immo (property, fair value EUR 411m). FY2025 adjusted PBT GS by segment: Belron EUR 518.0m, PHE EUR 181.6m, TVH EUR 72.0m, Moleskine minus EUR 8.0m; Automotive plus corporate about EUR 192m together (derived; the files do not split them, but corporate and unallocated was close to zero at this level in H1 2025, about minus EUR 0.5m, and about plus EUR 8.5m in H1 2026, both derived from the segment table, so most of the EUR 192m is Automotive, implying H2 2025 Automotive adjusted PBT of roughly EUR 80m). FY2025 group-share adjusted operating result by segment (derived from margins and stakes): Belron about EUR 778m (57 percent), PHE about EUR 243m (18 percent), Automotive about EUR 234m (17 percent), TVH about EUR 89m (7 percent), Moleskine about EUR 8m. H1 2026: Belron EUR 308.2m (+28.6 percent), PHE EUR 97.3m (+18.1 percent), TVH EUR 33.1m (+17.4 percent), Automotive EUR 36.4m (minus 66.6 percent), Moleskine minus EUR 1.1m, total EUR 482.4m (+6.6 percent reported, +8.4 percent at constant currency). H2 2025 was EUR 503.2m (derived: 955.6 less 452.4).
  4. The largest below-the-line item is Belron's debt. Belron net debt EUR 8,342.9m at 4.3x EBITDA (June 2026, junk-rated, down from 5.3x a year earlier and 5.5x at the 2024 peak); Belron adjusted finance costs EUR 506.2m at 100 percent in FY2025, about EUR 255m at group share. Group-share net finance and other costs were about EUR 411m in FY2025 (1,367.1 less 955.6). Interest relief from deleveraging is the largest single component of the consensus EPS growth path, and the largest single risk to it.
  5. Data conflicts already found in the files; do not carry them as facts. (a) FY2024 adjusted PBT GS: EUR 1,065.0m (earnings review, consistent with the "+3.8 percent at constant currency excluding extra interest" bridge) against EUR 921m (leadership digest); use 1,065.0. (b) Belron FY2025 leverage given as 4.5x, 4.6x and 4.73x. (c) Belron global share 17 percent (of a EUR 40bn market) or 19 percent. (d) The claim that D'Ieteren Automotive books about 80 percent of new-vehicle sales in the first half conflicts with FactSet's consolidated halves (H1 2025 EUR 4,052m against H2 2025 EUR 3,982m) and with an implied H2 2025 Automotive operating result of about EUR 119m (FY2025 4.7 percent margin on EUR 4,978.7m less an implied H1 2025 of about EUR 114.5m); do not model an 80/20 seasonality. (e) EUR 485.1m of H1 2026 free cash flow is attributed both to the group and to Belron. (f) FactSet's FY2026 Belron segment revenue consensus of EUR 6,256m implies a 7 percent fall against an H1 2026 run-rate that is up about 4.5 percent reported; treat it as stale or mis-based. (g) Automotive personnel costs "EUR 305m from EUR 185m" is unexplained and unverified. (h) The dividend for FY2025 is EUR 2.00 (company); FactSet's EUR 1.40 is a payment-period figure.

The pinned dashboard ratings at 2026-10-29: technical momentum D, sell-side earnings momentum F, valuation B; TSR/Valuation Master Rating D. The prior memo (2026-09-21, Triaging) was Neutral leaning bullish, park, with re-opening triggers at EUR 156 or the Belron liquidity event announced with terms.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Closing steer for the Consumer (not a question)

  • Build the model on adjusted PBT at group share by segment, then holding interest, tax at about 28 percent, a share count falling about 1 percent a year, and EPS on the brokers' adjusted basis.
  • Belron is the model: volume (2 to 4 percent), calibration penetration (52 percent toward 60 percent-plus by FY2028) and like-for-like price (at or slightly below inflation) give organic growth of 5 to 7 percent; incremental margins of 45 to 60 percent give a margin path toward 25 percent by FY2028; finance costs fall about EUR 45 to 50m a year at group share on the H1 2026 run-rate while leverage falls toward 3x; dollar translation is an explicit line (55 percent of revenue).
  • Automotive: a 400,000-unit market, share near 21 percent, margin about 2 percent in FY2026 recovering toward 3 percent, with explicit restructuring savings.
  • PHE: mid-single-digit organic, Spain consolidated with 49 percent minorities, margin about 9 to 10 percent, higher interest from the term-loan add-on.
  • TVH: low-to-mid-single-digit organic, margin recovering toward 15 percent, 40 percent share.
  • Carry one correlated stress case (refinancing +100 basis points, dollar minus 5 percent, tax 30 percent, Belron price and mix about 2 percent, Automotive margin about 2 percent): about 8 to 10 percent below the central FY2028 EPS.
  • Keep the Belron event out of the earnings model; it changes the multiple and, only if followed by distributions, the cash.
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: D'Ieteren Group (DIE-BE), a Belgian family-controlled investment holding whose segments are Belron (50.3 percent; global vehicle glass repair, replacement and ADAS camera recalibration, including Safelite in the US and Carglass and Autoglass in Europe), D'Ieteren Automotive (100 percent; exclusive importer and retailer of Volkswagen Group brands in Belgium), Parts Holding Europe (91 percent; independent car-parts distribution in France, Spain, Italy, Benelux and Ireland), TVH Parts (40 percent; aftermarket parts for forklifts and industrial equipment), Moleskine (100 percent; notebooks) and D'Ieteren Immo (property)

Request: T-P1

Peers: Boyd Group Services (Glass America and collision; closest listed glass and calibration competitor in the US); Driven Brands (Auto Glass Now; US glass network); Cary Group (private, Autoglass Clinic; European glass challenger); Inchcape (listed single-market vehicle distributor and importer, comparable to D'Ieteren Automotive); Bilia (listed Nordic dealer group with after-sales); LKQ Corporation, MEKO and Inter Cars (European independent parts distributors, comparable to PHE); Genuine Parts, RS Group and Diploma (industrial parts distributors, comparable to TVH); Kramp (private agricultural and industrial parts distributor) Inputs: How fixed or variable is the cost base of a vehicle glass repair and recalibration network (technician labour, glass, vans, branches, calibration equipment), and how did Belron's and Safelite's operating margins move through the 2020 traffic collapse and the 2024 to 2025 US claims-avoidance period? What incremental margin does an ADAS recalibration job carry once the equipment and trained technicians are in place, and what did Belron's "Transformation Program" actually deliver against what was promised, and over what time? What savings did D'Ieteren Automotive's proposed plan affecting 344 roles and site closures (announced September 2026) target, how long do Belgian consultation processes of this kind take, and what have Belgian car importers and dealer groups achieved from similar restructurings? How have importer and dealer margins behaved in past Belgian and European car-market downturns (2008 to 2009, 2012 to 2013, 2020), and what drop-through from revenue to operating profit did Inchcape and Bilia report? Which cost lines at independent parts distributors (transport, personnel, branch networks) proved cuttable in the 2025 parts-price deflation, and how did PHE hold its margin? What warehouse ramp-up and IT transformation costs did TVH incur in Poland and the US in 2025, and how quickly did comparable distributors recover margin after such transitions? What did the system-integrator fees reported among D'Ieteren's adjusting items in 2025 relate to, and are they recurring? Focus: How much of Belron's recent margin expansion is fixed-cost operating leverage that will persist as volume grows, and how much cost can genuinely be taken out of D'Ieteren Automotive's Belgian retail and distribution base, and how fast, as the market settles at a lower level.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Boyd Group Services (insurer-routed US glass and collision); Driven Brands (US cash-pay and insurer glass); Cary Group (private; European glass pricing); Safelite's insurer and administrator counterparts State Farm, Progressive, GEICO, Liberty Mutual, Travelers and USAA (buyers whose pricing policies set Belron's US prices); Inchcape and Bilia (vehicle distribution pricing); LKQ Corporation, Inter Cars and Auto Partner (European parts pricing); Genuine Parts and Diploma (industrial parts pricing) Operating conditions: US motor-insurance premium inflation and consumer claims avoidance through higher deductibles; insurer caps on ADAS recalibration reimbursement (State Farm, GEICO and Progressive in 2026) and documentation rules (USAA); glass input-cost inflation from tariffs and energy prices; a falling miles-driven or recessionary environment; tougher European glass pricing where insurer contracts are tendered country by country; a shrinking Belgian new-car market with Chinese-brand discounting and changes to company-car taxation; European car-parts price deflation (2025) and re-inflation (2026); an industrial and agricultural equipment downturn Inputs: What price did Belron and Safelite achieve and hold, separately from mix, in each year since 2019, and how much of Belron's reported price-and-mix growth (5.1 percent in FY2025 and 3.4 percent in H1 2026, company disclosures) is calibration mix rather than like-for-like price? How do NAGS-indexed and semi-annual insurer contracts work in practice, what is the lag before glass cost increases reach prices, and have any insurers changed the indexation since January 2026? What are current US and European prices for static, dynamic and dual calibrations, how have they moved since 2024, and what share of Belron's revenue is calibration? What exactly have State Farm, GEICO, Progressive and USAA changed in calibration billing in 2025 and 2026, and has any large insurer moved volume away from Safelite or reopened its third-party administration contracts? How does Safelite's cash-pay premium over regional competitors hold when deductibles rise? How did Belgian importers' and dealers' pricing and margins respond to the 2026 removal of electric-vehicle subsidies and to Chinese-brand discounting, and what discounts is Volkswagen funding? How much of PHE's and TVH's price increases since 2023 were passed through and held? Focus: Whether Belron's calibration-led pricing power survives the move by large US insurers to regulate calibration billing, and at what pace like-for-like price can grow from here, with the European price gap as the reference case.

Request: T-P3 (only where your answers show this report would be useful for this company)

Why requested: D'Ieteren's earnings path depends on interacting loops (calibration content, scale and insurer routing reinforcing Belron's share; deleveraging cutting finance costs and enabling a listing; volume decline deleveraging Automotive's cost base; and insurer pushback balancing Belron's margin expansion) whose direction and strength the model must capture. Peers: Boyd Group Services and Driven Brands (US glass networks on the other side of the same loops); Cary Group (private; European challenger); Inchcape and Bilia (distributors facing the volume-decline loop); Inter Cars and LKQ Corporation (scale and cost loops in parts distribution); GBL, Sofina and Exor (holding companies whose discounts have widened or narrowed after asset listings or disposals) Inputs: Does rising ADAS calibration content per windscreen (Belron calibration penetration 52.1 percent in H1 2026 against 45.9 percent a year earlier, company disclosure, 9 September 2026) raise the equipment and training barrier enough to push independent shops to sublet or exit, and is that shifting insurer-routed volume and share to Belron, step by step and with evidence? Does Safelite's role as State Farm's glass claims administrator from July 2025 increase its share of routed jobs, and does that scale feed back into purchasing terms and lower cost per job? Does the margin Belron earns on calibration provoke insurer caps, documentation rules or re-tendering (a balancing loop), and is there evidence of this since January 2026? Does Belron's deleveraging (4.3x net debt to EBITDA in June 2026, company disclosure) lower its borrowing cost through repricing and ratings, freeing more cash for further deleveraging, and what would stop it (rates, a dividend recapitalisation, acquisitions)? At D'Ieteren Automotive, does falling volume reduce scale with Volkswagen, dealer viability and share in a self-reinforcing way, and where has a comparable loop stopped for other single-manufacturer importers? Have past listings of a holding company's main asset narrowed the holding discount, and only when followed by distributions or buybacks? Focus: Which of these loops is strongest over the next 18 months to four years, and in particular whether the insurer pushback on calibration price is strong enough to stall the reinforcing share-and-margin loop at Belron.

Request: T-P4 (only where your answers show this report would be useful for this company)

Why requested: D'Ieteren's earnings per share depend heavily on items below the operating line or between the subsidiaries and the holding (Belron's finance costs, currency translation, tax, minorities and the share count) that the company does not guide. Peers: Boyd Group Services (US glass; USD exposure and financing); Inchcape (distributor with translation exposure and buybacks); LKQ Corporation (leveraged parts distributor); GBL, Sofina and Exor (holding companies; discount, buybacks and distributions from listed stakes); Cary Group (private; leveraged glass buyout comparable) Inputs: Belron's debt stack after the 2024 recapitalisation and the July 2025 and January 2026 term-loan repricings: amounts, currencies, maturities, fixed and floating mix, hedging, ratings and rating triggers, and the interest saving each turn of deleveraging brings; Belron's dividend policy to its shareholders once leverage falls, and what any listing prospectus or lender disclosure says about it; D'Ieteren's adjusted effective tax rate by segment and why the reported consolidated rate (43.3 percent in FY2025, company) differs; the translation sensitivity of D'Ieteren's adjusted profit before tax, group share, to the US dollar and sterling (company disclosures cite a 10 percent dollar move as about EUR 75m of group-share EBITDA) and what the sell-side assumes for EUR/USD; minorities inside PHE after buying 51 percent of Polaris and Regueira (May 2026) and the cost of the EUR 200m term-loan add-on at Euribor plus 300 basis points; the share count across D'Ieteren's capital and participating shares, the pace of the EUR 100m buyback launched in December 2025 and the SPDG stake disposal over five years; holding-level costs and interest on corporate net debt; the size of recurring adjusting items (other adjusting items EUR 132m in 2025, company) and purchase-price amortisation excluded from adjusted earnings; how far apart management guidance (adjusted profit before tax, group share, growth at a low-to-mid-single-digit rate at constant currency for 2026) and individual brokers' 2027 and 2028 assumptions are on each of these items. Focus: How much of the expected earnings-per-share growth to 2028 depends on Belron's falling finance costs, and how sensitive that is to refinancing rates, the dollar and the tax rate.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 31 October 2026, 11:51 to 12:10 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
D'Ieteren Group (DIE-BE): second set of questions, the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years

Prepared 2026-10-31 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Price EUR 171.3 (2026-10-29). All figures in EUR millions unless stated; "GS" means group share (Belron at 50.3 percent, TVH at 40 percent, the rest as consolidated, minorities netted); "PBT GS" means adjusted profit before tax, group share, the company's guided line; EPS is on the brokers' adjusted basis (FY2025 EUR 12.95). Figures labelled "derived" are my arithmetic from company segment data; figures labelled "my estimate" or "base case" are forecasts.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading guide for the Consumer: the seven things that decide this model

  1. The base case in one line. PBT GS EUR 955.6m (FY2025) to about EUR 992m (FY2026, +3.8 percent reported, about +5.5 percent at constant currency), EUR 1,127m (FY2027, +13.6 percent), EUR 1,266m (FY2028, +12.3 percent), EUR 1,371m (FY2029) and EUR 1,461m (FY2030). EPS EUR 13.63, 15.60, 17.68, 19.32 and 20.77 against consensus medians of EUR 13.40, 15.56, 17.93 and 21.55 (FY2029, two brokers). My path is at consensus for FY2026 to FY2028 within 2 percent, slightly above in FY2026 and slightly below in FY2028, and well below the two-broker FY2029 figure.
  2. The single most important correction to the first set. The first set (C-49, C-76, C-84) and the prior memo treat finance-cost relief at Belron as the largest single component of EPS growth (about 40 percent of FY2025 to FY2028 growth); one AlphaSense report says "about half" (that figure is the report writer's own judgement, not evidence). On my build, Belron's finance-cost relief is about EUR 81m of the EUR 310m rise in PBT GS from FY2025 to FY2028 (26 percent), and about EUR 50m of that EUR 81m falls in FY2026 and is already in the H1 2026 run-rate. For FY2027 and FY2028, the years in which consensus has +16 and +15 percent, relief is only about EUR 31m of EUR 274m (11 percent). The prior memo's "4.4 points a year below the operating line" is about 2.4 points of relief plus about 2.1 points of pure gearing (a fixed finance bill turns 5.3 percent operating growth into 7.4 percent PBT growth). Consequence: the forward growth is operational (Belron's operating result, an Automotive floor, PHE), so operating risk matters more and rate risk less than the first set implied.
  3. Belron is still the model. Belron supplies about 72 percent of the FY2025 to FY2028 PBT GS rise through operations alone. My Belron path: revenue EUR 6,722m to EUR 7,860m by FY2028 (organic about 6.8, 6.0 and 5.5 percent in FY2026 to FY2028), margin 23.0 percent to 25.3 percent by FY2028 and 25.8 percent by FY2030, incremental margins falling from 43 percent (FY2026) to about 30 percent (FY2030) because price and mix fades from 5.1 percent (FY2025) to about 2 percent as calibration penetration plateaus and insurers push back.
  4. FY2026 is a finance-cost year, FY2027 is an operations year. In FY2026 Belron's finance relief (about +EUR 50m GS) offsets most of Automotive's collapse (about minus EUR 140m of PBT GS). In FY2027 the growth needs Belron's operating result up about 9 percent, Automotive's margin up from about 2.1 to 2.6 percent on restructuring savings that arrive from mid-2027, and a full year of Spain at PHE.
  5. The cone is narrow for FY2026, moderate for FY2027 and wider and skewed down for FY2028. FY2026 EPS EUR 13.45 to 13.85 (H1 is known); FY2027 EUR 14.70 to 16.30; FY2028 EUR 16.00 to 18.60 (the correlated stress case, minus 9 percent, against a bull case of plus 5 percent).
  6. How to enter this company into the model. The forecast form has no line for the equity-accounted Belron and TVH. I enter consolidated revenue and EBITDA (Automotive, PHE, Moleskine, Corporate and Immo) and put the share of Belron's and TVH's adjusted PBT GS, together with consolidated finance costs and minorities, into "Net financial items", so that EBITDA less depreciation plus net financial items equals PBT GS and the model's EPS matches the brokers' basis at a 28 percent tax rate. The model's own free cash flow will therefore be overstated (it will treat the equity-accounted profit as cash); my cross-check free cash flow counts only the dividends Belron and TVH pay to D'Ieteren. The Part 4 notes of the forecast form set this out in full.
  7. Data conflicts I resolved, and the ones I carry. (a) The PHE term-loan add-on is at Euribor plus 250 basis points (company half-year report, 9 September 2026), not plus 300 as in the first set's sources. (b) Belron already pays dividends to its shareholders (EUR 193.5m in H1 2026, of which EUR 102.6m to D'Ieteren; EUR 211m in H1 2025): the first set's reading that Belron "could resume distributions" at under 3x is wrong in kind; the channel exists, its size is the variable. (c) Belron's derived finance costs and other items below the operating result are EUR 254m (H1 2025), EUR 262m (H2 2025) and EUR 208m (H1 2026) at 100 percent, an 18 percent fall that the company confirms (AlphaSense: net finance costs down 18.2 percent). (d) The Corporate, D'Ieteren Immo and Moleskine lines cannot be separated reliably from the files (the AlphaSense report gives Corporate at minus EUR 8.5m in H1 2026 against minus EUR 16.7m a year earlier; my derived residual of Corporate, Immo and Moleskine together is plus EUR 7.4m against minus EUR 6.9m); I model them as one residual line. (e) Calibration is about 8 percent of Belron's revenue (Bernstein, September 2026), which changes how much of Belron's price and mix is calibration (see P-04).

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue growth. FY2026 minus 1.5 percent: Automotive minus 10.4 percent (H1 2026 minus 10.8 percent on deliveries minus 8.3 percent, share 21.5 percent, a Belgian market heading to the company's 400,000-unit plateau, adverse price and mix) against PHE +13.5 percent (organic about 5.5 percent plus seven months of Polaris and Regueira, about EUR 340m of annual sales, consolidated from 29 May 2026). FY2027 +2.1 percent: Automotive minus 2.9 percent (end of 100 percent deductibility for corporate electric vehicles, mobility budgets), PHE +8.8 percent (full year of Spain, organic 4.5 percent). FY2028 to FY2030 +2.4 to 2.9 percent: Automotive +1 to 1.5 percent on a stable market and share near 21 percent; PHE +4 to 4.6 percent (organic about 4 percent and small bolt-ons); parts prices +1.5 to 2 percent a year, passed through.
  • EBITDA margin. Built as the consolidated adjusted operating result plus depreciation excluding acquired-intangible amortisation. The FY2026 dip (7.80 percent) is Automotive's margin at about 2.1 percent (H1 2026 2.1 percent against 4.5 percent; a 24 percent decremental margin); the rise to 8.45 percent (FY2027) and 8.82 percent (FY2028) is the Automotive restructuring (344 roles, site closures, marketing and consulting cuts; about EUR 30m to 35m gross a year, about half in FY2027 after Belgian consultation, the run-rate in FY2028) and PHE's margin rising from 9.2 to 9.6 percent with Spanish procurement synergies. I do not reach the company's Automotive ambition of about 4 percent by FY2028 (pre-pandemic about 3 percent; price and mix still negative).
  • Depreciation and amortisation. About EUR 205m in FY2025 excluding acquired-intangible amortisation (FactSet's total EUR 231m less PHE's EUR 25.9m), plus Spain from FY2026 and a slowly rising asset base.
  • Net financial items. See the bridge below. The large positive figure is the share of Belron's and TVH's adjusted profit before tax. Belron's share grows from EUR 518m to EUR 638m in FY2026 (operating result about EUR 1,685m at 100 percent, +9 percent; finance and other costs down from about EUR 516m to EUR 416m at 100 percent after the October 2025 step-down, the January 2026 repricing and deleveraging; derived H1 2026 finance costs EUR 208m against EUR 254m, an 18 percent fall the company confirms), then by about EUR 70m to 95m a year as the margin moves toward 25.3 percent by FY2028 and finance costs fall by about EUR 20m to 30m a year at 100 percent. Belron's price and mix fades from 5.1 percent (FY2025) to about 2 percent by FY2029, its volume runs at 2.5 to 3 percent, and its incremental margin falls from about 43 percent to about 30 percent, because US insurers (State Farm, GEICO, Progressive, Travelers, Liberty Mutual) are regulating calibration and glass pricing at rolling renewals. TVH's margin recovers from 13.4 percent toward 15 percent as warehouse ramp-up costs fade, below its 16 percent target.
  • Tax rate. 28 percent on the adjusted basis (FY2025 implied 28.2 percent). The reported consolidated rates (43.3 percent FY2025, 55.4 percent FY2024) are distorted by equity accounting and must not be used.
  • Dividend per share. EUR 2.00 declared for FY2025 (FactSet's EUR 1.40 is a payment-period figure); a progressive policy at about 15 percent of adjusted EPS.
  • Share count. No changes other than the buyback. The buyback (EUR 100m programme from December 2025; 337,711 shares bought by 15 September 2026; treasury 2.7 percent) runs at about EUR 80m in FY2026 and EUR 90m to 100m a year after; average ordinary shares outstanding 52.4m, 52.0m, 51.55m, 51.1m and 50.65m on my estimate. The 5m participating shares are outside the EPS denominator.
  • Capital spending. FactSet's fixed-asset and intangible capital spending was EUR 112m in FY2025 and EUR 61.6m in H1 2026; I add Spain and a slowly growing base; consensus is EUR 118m to 126m.
  • Working capital. Year-end balance about 8.0 percent of revenue in FY2024 and FY2025 (H1 balances are seasonally higher: 14.3 percent at June 2026). Spain (a parts distributor at about 20 percent of sales in working capital) lifts the year-end ratio to about 8.8 percent in FY2026 and 9.0 percent from FY2027.
  • Lease payments. Mainly PHE's network (D'Ieteren Immo owns most Automotive sites); my estimate, not disclosed in the files.
  • Acquisitions less disposals. FY2026: EUR 156m in H1 (FactSet, mainly Spain) and about EUR 20m of bolt-ons in H2. FY2027: EUR 194m for the put option over the 9 percent of PHE's holding company, which matures at the end of 2026 (company, H1 2026 report), and about EUR 50m of bolt-ons; if the put is not exercised, FY2027 is about minus EUR 50m. FY2028 onward: about EUR 60m a year of PHE and Automotive bolt-ons. No disposal of Moleskine or Immo property is assumed. The puts over the 49 percent of Polaris and Regueira (within EUR 307.4m of minority puts) are not assumed to be exercised inside the horizon.
  • Buybacks. As under the share count above.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 guidance: adjusted PBT GS to grow at a low-to-mid-single-digit rate at constant currency (EUR/USD 1.17), set in March 2026 and confirmed on 9 September 2026. That implies about EUR 975m to EUR 1,005m at constant currency. My EUR 992m is +3.8 percent reported and about +5.5 percent at constant currency: the top of the range, in line with the company's record of beating its own guidance in FY2022 to FY2025. Consensus EPS EUR 13.40 implies about EUR 980m; Deutsche Bank EUR 984.2m; Jefferies EUR 1,013.6m.
  • Segment guidance for FY2026: Belron mid-to-high-single-digit revenue growth with margin progressing (mine: +4.8 percent reported, about 6.8 percent organic, margin 23.9 percent); PHE mid-single-digit organic plus consolidation at about 9.1 percent margin (mine: 9.2 percent); TVH low-to-mid-single-digit organic with a declining operating result (mine: about flat, after H1 2026 PBT GS +17 percent); Automotive negative top line and material margin decline (mine: minus 10.4 percent, 2.1 percent margin).
  • FY2028 ambitions (Investor Day, May 2025, reaffirmed): Belron margin above 25 percent and organic growth above 5 percent (mine: 25.3 percent and 5.5 percent, met); TVH 16 percent (mine 14.5 percent, below); Automotive about 4 percent (mine 3.0 percent, below); Moleskine 15 percent (mine about 7 percent, below); group mid-single-digit sales growth with continuous margin improvement (mine: group-share revenue about +3.5 percent a year, below; margin improving from FY2027, met).
  • FY2027: not yet guided. The first guidance under the new chief executive (Eric Machiels, from December 2026) comes on 10 March 2027. My FY2027 PBT GS of +13.6 percent is likely to be above the way the company frames it, given its habit of low-to-mid-single-digit framing.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Consensus EPS basis. Brokers' adjusted EPS = adjusted PBT GS x (1 - about 28 percent) / average ordinary shares outstanding. FY2025: EUR 955.6m x 0.718 / about 53.1m = EUR 12.95. This is the basis of my cross-check EPS.
  • How the model's lines map onto it. Revenue, EBITDA and depreciation are the consolidated perimeter (Automotive, PHE, Moleskine, Corporate, Immo). EBITDA minus depreciation (excluding acquired-intangible amortisation) is the consolidated adjusted operating result: EUR 407m, 460m, 505m, 532m and 550m. "Net financial items" is everything between that and PBT GS: EUR 585m, 667m, 761m, 839m and 911m, made of (a) the share of Belron's and TVH's adjusted PBT GS (EUR 713m, 814m, 911m, 989m and 1,061m), less (b) consolidated finance costs, PHE minorities (9 percent at the holding company; 49 percent of the Spanish companies), Automotive's below-operating items and the Corporate, Immo and Moleskine residual (minus EUR 128m, 147m, 150m, 150m and 150m). EBITDA minus depreciation plus net financial items therefore equals PBT GS (EUR 992m, 1,127m, 1,266m, 1,371m and 1,461m), and at a 28 percent tax rate the model's net income equals adjusted net income GS and its EPS equals the brokers'-basis EPS (subject to the model's own share count).
  • Important for the model's free cash flow. Because "Net financial items" includes equity-accounted profit, any free cash flow the model derives from net income will be overstated by roughly that profit after the model's 28 percent tax, less the dividends Belron and TVH actually pay to D'Ieteren: about EUR 395m in FY2026 (EUR 713m x 0.72 less EUR 118m), rising to about EUR 485m in FY2030 (EUR 1,061m x 0.72 less EUR 279m). My cross-check free cash flow below is built correctly and should be preferred.
  • EBITDA bases. FactSet's history line is operating EBITDA (EUR 622m in FY2025, 7.7 percent); the brokers' adjusted basis for FY2025 is EUR 681m (8.5 percent); my company-adjusted basis (consolidated adjusted operating result plus depreciation excluding acquired-intangible amortisation) is about EUR 714m (8.9 percent). FY2026 on my basis is EUR 617m (7.80 percent) against consensus EUR 627m (7.9 percent). Do not compare my margins with the FactSet history line as growth.
  • Revenue bases. FactSet consolidated revenue (EUR 8,034m in FY2025) is not the company's headline group-share revenue (EUR 12,082.1m), which adds 50.3 percent of Belron and 40 percent of TVH: my group-share revenue is EUR 12,152m, 12,548m, 13,017m, 13,472m and 13,895m. FactSet's FY2026 Belron segment consensus (EUR 6,256m) is stale or mis-based and is not used.
  • Reported against adjusted. Reported basic EPS (EUR 8.04 in FY2025) excludes purchase-price amortisation (2025: Belron EUR 31.4m, TVH EUR 44.5m, PHE EUR 25.9m at 100 percent), other adjusting items (Belron EUR 131.9m in 2025, including EUR 44.3m of system-integrator fees, EUR 31.0m of warehouse closures and EUR 19.3m of restructuring), impairments (Automotive goodwill EUR 47.2m in H1 2026) and the tax on these. Expect reported EPS to stay 35 to 40 percent below adjusted EPS.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Earnings per share is on the brokers' adjusted basis (PBT GS x 0.72 / average ordinary shares). Free cash flow is free cash flow to D'Ieteren: consolidated EBITDA less cash tax, net interest paid, lease payments, capital spending, the change in working capital and cash restructuring and other adjusting items, plus dividends received from Belron and TVH; before acquisitions, buybacks and dividends paid. Belron dividends to D'Ieteren assumed at EUR 103m, 110m, 140m, 200m and 260m; TVH at EUR 15m to 19m.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 3 November 2026, 08:26 to 08:48 UK, Fable, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
D'Ieteren Group (DIE-BE): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and what guidance, the sell-side and the narrative already reflect

Prepared 2026-11-03 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Price EUR 171.3 (2026-10-29).
